Bergomi: "Spence-Dumfries, le differenze. Sull'Inter continuo a pensare che..."

Intervenuto ai microfoni di Sky Sport, Beppe Bergomi ha parlato così di Spence, MVP dell'UEFA contro il Feyenoord:

"Spence e Dumfries sono giocatori diversi, Dumfries dribbling zero, forza fisica tanta, Spence è più veloce di Dumfries ma la differenza è negli assist e nei gol. Anche Dumfries quando è arrivato ha avuto bisogno di tempo per adattarsi, prima giocava a 4 e poi è diventato quello che è diventato. Se Spence alza la qualità della giocata, del cross, dell'assist… Non ha quel colpo di testa, quella chiusura dell'azione che aveva l'olandese. Dei tre inglesi, Spence è quello con più margine di miglioramento e a livello europeo dove ti lasciano campo da attaccare diventa perfetto"

"In questi sei anni, l'Inter è sempre stata brava a ripartire e rialzarsi dopo una delusione forte, una reazione c'è stata, ora deve dare continuità. Continuo a pensare che la fragilità dell'Inter nel derby è conclamata, la velocità di alcuni giocatori del Milan come Moreira e Pulisic mettono a nudo le difficoltà dell'Inter, anche la fisicità di Rabiot. Siccome credo molto nelle energie, il Milan aveva fame e si è vista la differenza"

A tiny memory‑handling bug in the musl‑specific OpenSSH code path has turned millions of containers, edge routers, and IoT devices into credential‑theft machines. Within minutes of the CVE‑2026‑YYYY disclosure, Reddit’s r/sysadmin, Hacker News, and Google Trends saw a 300 %+ surge in searches for “OpenSSH musl CVE‑2026”. If your fleet runs Alpine, OpenWrt, or any image that bundles OpenSSH compiled against musl libc, you are already in the attacker’s cross‑hairs.

This guide cuts through the noise: we’ll show you how to detect, prove, and fix the vulnerability on Linux hosts, Docker containers, and OpenWrt routers. You’ll get a ready‑to‑run detection script, a GitHub Action for CI pipelines, a patch‑vs‑backport matrix, and a printable checklist you can hand to your ops team today.

If the script prints “VULNERABLE”, you are running the affected OpenSSH binary.

Add the script to your CI pipeline (see the GitHub Action below) to catch new builds before they hit production.

Note: These settings stop the most common exploitation paths but do not fix the underlying memory leak. Deploy the patched package as soon as possible.

Push the image to your registry and roll it out with your usual orchestration tool.

The workflow fails the build if a vulnerable OpenSSH binary is detected, preventing accidental releases.

CVE‑2026‑YYYY is a supply‑chain‑level credential‑theft bug that spreads through the most popular lightweight Linux base images. The window for exploitation is already open; the longer you wait, the more likely an attacker will harvest your private keys and pivot into your internal network.

By integrating the detection script, CI gate, and patching steps outlined above, you can eliminate the risk across servers, containers, and edge devices in a single, repeatable workflow.

Stay vigilant, keep your musl‑based OpenSSH up‑to‑date, and let the community know when you’ve secured your fleet.

Local LLMs vs Cloud AI: Choosing the Right Solution for...

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at veduis.com on Nov 6

The explosion of large language model capabilities has created a fundamental choice for small businesses: rely on cloud AI services like ChatGPT and Claude, or run AI models locally on owned hardware. Each approach carries distinct implications for privacy, cost, performance, and operational complexity.

This comparison examines both options through the lens of small business requirements, providing the framework needed to make an informed decision based on specific circumstances rather than general hype.

Before diving into comparisons, clarity on what each approach actually means helps frame the discussion.

Cloud AI refers to large language models hosted by companies like OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and Microsoft. Users send queries over the internet to remote servers where powerful hardware processes requests and returns responses. The model itself never touches the user's device.

Local LLMs are AI models that run entirely on hardware the user owns or controls. The model files download to local storage, and all processing happens on local CPUs, GPUs, or specialized AI accelerators. No data leaves the local environment during inference.

For many small businesses, privacy concerns drive the local LLM consideration more than any other factor.

When using cloud AI services, every prompt and response passes through third-party infrastructure:

OpenAI's business tier and API usage exclude data from training by default, but enterprise agreements provide stronger guarantees. Consumer ChatGPT usage may contribute to model training unless users opt out. Similar variations exist across providers.

For businesses handling client confidential information, healthcare data, legal documents, or proprietary processes, cloud AI usage requires careful evaluation of provider terms and potential liability.

Local deployment eliminates third-party data exposure entirely:

A law firm using AI to summarize case documents gains significant advantage from local deployment. Client privilege concerns that might prohibit cloud AI usage disappear when processing happens entirely on firm-controlled hardware.

Cost comparisons require honest accounting of all expenses, not just subscription fees.

Moderate usage with ChatGPT Team: $25 × 5 × 12 = $1,500/year

Heavy API usage (1M tokens/month): Approximately $300-500/month = $3,600-6,000/year

Local deployment requires upfront hardware investment with minimal ongoing costs.

A $2,000 hardware investment for medium-capability local LLM breaks even against $125/month cloud spending in 16 months. After break-even, local operation costs only electricity.

Raw capability differences between cloud and local options have narrowed significantly but remain relevant.

The largest, most capable models remain cloud-exclusive. GPT-4, Claude 3 Opus, and Gemini Ultra offer reasoning capabilities that exceed what runs efficiently on consumer hardware.

Cloud providers operate massive GPU clusters improved for inference. Response times typically range from 1-10 seconds for most queries, with streaming output beginning almost immediately.

Open-source models have improved dramatically. Meta's Llama 3 family, Mistral models, and community fine-tunes offer impressive capabilities:

Local performance depends entirely on hardware:

Larger models run proportionally slower. A 70B model might generate 10-20 tokens/second on high-end consumer hardware.

For businesses ready to examine local deployment, these tools provide the most accessible entry points.

Ollama provides the easiest path to running local LLMs. A single command downloads and runs models with sensible defaults.

Ollama exposes a local API compatible with many applications:

LM Studio offers a graphical interface for users preferring visual model management over command line.

Install Ollama or LM Studio on individual workstations. Each user maintains their own model installation. Simple but redundant.

Deploy models on a dedicated server accessible across the local network. All team members access the same instance:

Use local LLMs for sensitive processing while maintaining cloud access for tasks requiring peak capability. Route requests based on content sensitivity and complexity.

Different business scenarios favor different approaches.

Processing internal documents, contracts, meeting notes, and reports works excellently with local models. No external transmission, consistent performance, and 7B-13B models handle summarization well.

Developers working with proprietary codebases benefit from local code models like CodeLlama. IDE integrations like Continue work smoothly with local Ollama backends.

Parsing information from documents, emails, or databases involves potentially sensitive data. Local processing eliminates exposure concerns while capable models handle structured extraction tasks effectively.

Generating response drafts for customer inquiries allows local AI to help with routine communications while human review ensures quality before sending.

Tasks requiring deep reasoning, multi-step problem solving, or synthesis across broad knowledge domains benefit from the largest cloud models.

Marketing copy, blog posts, and creative writing often benefit from the nuanced language capabilities of frontier models.

Occasional research queries where peak capability matters more than privacy work well with cloud services.

Translation and multilingual content generation typically perform better on large cloud models trained on extensive multilingual data.

Use this framework to guide the cloud vs. local decision for specific business contexts.

The local LLM landscape evolves rapidly, with several trends worth monitoring.

New AI accelerators from Apple, AMD, and dedicated AI chip companies continue improving local inference performance. Hardware purchased today will run future improved models faster.

Research in model compression, quantization, and architecture improvements steadily closes the capability gap between what runs locally and cloud-exclusive models.

Commercial offerings for enterprise local AI deployment are emerging, providing managed solutions for businesses wanting local benefits with reduced operational overhead.

Increasing data privacy regulation may force certain industries toward local processing regardless of capability tradeoffs.

The choice between local and cloud AI is not permanent. Many businesses start with cloud services for immediate capability access, then migrate specific workloads to local deployment as use cases mature and privacy requirements clarify. The key is making an informed initial decision while remaining flexible as both technology and business needs evolve.

Related reading: NVIDIA Flare for local inference.

How we moved our agents from personal terminals into team channels

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at agentconnect.md

In six months, a team of under ten went from editor autocomplete to about thirty agents with standing jobs, from cluster checks and code review to releases, fork maintenance and customer support, plus the permissions and context sharing around them.

Over the past few months, AI has fundamentally changed how our team works. Much of our recurring work is now genuinely automated by agents: the daily cluster inspection and alert analysis, pull request review, maintenance of an upstream fork, release notes, and answering questions in customer groups. We are an engineering team of fewer than ten people.

A year ago, "using AI" meant an editor with AI in it: completions, rewriting a function, explaining unfamiliar code. Everyone used it, and nothing about how the team worked changed. The output was still a diff on someone's machine. Nobody else saw the conversation, and there was nothing in it worth seeing.

We did not build anything around it. The models were not yet at the point where you could hand them a task and walk away.

The turning point was Claude Opus 4.6. It was the first model we used that was worth building around: give it a task and it runs commands, reads the results and keeps going. Within weeks Claude Code and Codex were everyone's daily tools.

Then people started giving them work other than code. A production error: let an agent take the first look. An upstream release: let an agent upgrade our fork. Inspect the cluster every morning. Take customer questions. Within a month every engineer had accumulated a pile of their own glue: shell scripts, cron entries, a bot token in a dotfile, a prompt that stitched together the context the agent needed.

The first was duplication. When we compared what each of us had written, it was nearly the same: how messages get in and out, how to schedule, where credentials live, how to assemble context. Everyone had done it once, and each copy only ran on its owner's machine.

The second was lost context. The most useful part of a session is usually not the final diff but the process in the middle: what was tried, what failed, why this fix over that one, which dashboard had the answer. All of it sat in one person's terminal and was gone when the session ended. When someone else needed it, the only way to get it was a meeting.

In June we tried the most direct approach: a set of Slack bots built on the open-source OpenACP, changed so that each thread in an existing channel became one session.

Someone mentioned an agent in an incident thread, the rest of the team watched what it was checking, and asked follow-up questions in the same thread. Colleagues outside engineering started using the bots too; a mention in Slack is all it takes.

Then came two weeks of patches, all about threads: carrying the earlier thread into a mid-thread mention, backfilling messages the bot had missed, stopping bots from triggering each other in a loop. The software had no concept of "a conversation with several people and several bots in it", and we were bolting one on.

One platform app with several agents behind it, instead of one process, one Slack app and one set of tokens per bot

Separate identities for several users, instead of one shared login on one machine

A real control plane, instead of a bot that ran operational shell commands from the chat

Versioned daemons with controlled upgrades

Sessions that survive a restart, each with its own workspace, instead of one directory shared by every thread

Conversations and threads as core concepts, not adapter patches

We also tried replacing the whole thing with Hermes. Its demo covered most of what we wanted: loops, proactive messages, a kanban board and memory, all built in. We moved a rebase bot onto it and ran it for a day. The biggest problem was that it had no team layer: who may use which agent, who may see its sessions, which repositories and tools an agent may touch. There was no permission model; the whole thing assumed one user. Beyond that, its own harness and Claude Code ran interleaved inside one task, and without the coding agent's cache and resume, every turn re-sent the full context, so latency and cost were both high. When something failed, we could not tell which layer had failed. This describes the version we tried at the time; Hermes has changed a great deal since.

Two decisions from that time have not changed: permissions are part of the platform, not a feature to add later; and we keep the harness engineers already run locally, so that any behaviour seen in a channel can be reproduced in a terminal.

We built the connecting layer ourselves, on ACP, and open-sourced it a month later as AgentConnect.

The production organization was created on a Sunday in July. By Tuesday six agents were running, the same jobs people had been running from personal scripts, now each with a name, a channel and a machine. Today:

About thirty agents: one generalist, the rest specialists

Running on seven of our own machines plus a managed pool

Connected to Slack, Telegram, Discord and Linear

The generalist is connected to the development channel, direct messages and Linear. It can check out the team's repositories on demand, and anyone can mention it. Small changes are done right in the Slack thread: describe the problem, it edits the code, opens a pull request and runs the checks, and a person glances at it in the thread and merges, without opening a terminal.

Each specialist owns one kind of work, with its own repositories, tools, channels and schedules, and permissions scoped to that work.

Design documents and meetings. One agent owns documentation. Design discussions mention it in the development channel; it reads the current system and the existing design documents and proposes a path, colleagues add constraints in the thread, and it revises. Once the discussion settles, it opens a pull request that writes the conclusion into the matching design document under docs/, reviewed together with the implementation. Every meeting is transcribed. We do not use the transcription service's own summaries; the raw transcript goes into a separate repository, the agent writes the summary there, and then updates the related issues and design documents in the other repositories.

The documentation repository is organized by project, and each project has two parts, design documents and meetings:

Inspection and alerts. One agent walks the cluster every morning and posts a report to the alerts channel. A few others each watch one class of service in a business area for latency and versions. When an alert lands in the channel, the on-call agent picks it up in the same thread: it checks Grafana metrics, looks at pods and logs, and compares against recent changes in the repository. Kubernetes and Grafana are MCP services, registered once at the organization level and enabled per agent. How each class of event should be handled is written as a skill in the repository: which metrics to check first, the common causes, what it may handle on its own and when it must call a person. When the procedure changes, the skill changes, and every on-call agent uses the new one from the next run. Whether it is an incident is the engineer's call.

Cross-model code review. Every pull request gets a first pass from the review bot, which leaves inline comments and posts its own check. The same review bot can switch runtime and model per pull request, and different models notice different things. Running on Codex, it reproduced a bug in one pull request: editing a scheduled task's time before saving dropped the saved task's identity. It requested changes, and approved once the fix landed. Running on Claude, it approved another pull request and listed exactly what it had not verified and was leaving to CI. For a deeper look, someone mentions a reviewer on a stronger model.

Releases. Every merge to main produces a release candidate; a stable release points the release branch at a chosen commit. What happens after the release is the agent's job: confirm the release succeeded, read the pull requests between the two stable versions, group related pull requests into one feature entry, call out behaviour changes and upgrade notes separately, write that as the Summary at the top of the release page, and then comment on and close each issue the release fully fixed. The Summary on v2.8.0 came from this; the Features list below it is the generated changelog. The procedure is a skill, so the rules are the same whichever agent runs it.

The upstream fork. We maintain a fork of an upstream runtime adapter: the upstream tag plus a pile of our own patches. The patches carry logic, so when upstream refactors, the job is not resolving a few lines of conflict. It is understanding what upstream changed and what our patch was for, then re-implementing the intent on the new base. The whole thing goes to a coding agent: an upstream release or a schedule starts it, it reads both sides' changes, decides which patches are still needed, which upstream has absorbed and can be dropped, and which need rewriting, makes the changes in its own worktree, runs typecheck, tests and build, opens a pull request, and posts what needs a human judgement to the channel. That repository now has fourteen rebase branches, one per upstream version, with the check results beside each. Merging and publishing are done by a person.

Customer group support. A support agent answers questions in customers' Telegram groups and escalates what it cannot answer to an engineering channel in Slack. The groups get a lot of spam, so each message is scored first; only messages under the threshold reach the support agent, and the rest go to a moderator agent. Replies about a specific account are confirmed by a person before they go out.

The generalist can reach many repositories and sits in public channels, private channels and direct messages at once, so permissions cannot be skipped. We handle them in three layers.

The agent itself: who may change its configuration, which repositories and tools it may touch, who may use it in which channels, and which agents may call it or be called by it, each set separately.

Sessions: who may read a session follows the membership of the place the conversation happened. A session in a private channel is readable only by people currently in that channel; whoever leaves the channel can no longer read it. Direct messages stay private. There is no separate list to maintain.

Memory is layered by where the conversation happened: what was said somewhere is remembered there. What an agent learns in a public channel goes into the shared layer, a private or external group gets its own layer, and a direct message gets that person's layer. When it answers, the agent loads only the memory the current conversation is allowed to read.

Operations that need approval send an approval card by direct message to the agent's editors, rather than letting anyone in the channel approve.

What an agent produces has to be kept somewhere the team and the other agents can find later, with access still controlled by where it came from.

Design documents live in the same repository as the code, under docs/, with implementation status and links to issues and pull requests. The project instructions file says where the documents are and that a change to a design and its implementation go in the same pull request. The product repository has gone from 35 design documents at open-sourcing to 100 now. A reviewer reads the linked design document directly instead of asking the author to explain it in a meeting. Anything beyond a single repository, runbooks and architecture decisions, goes into the organization's knowledge library: owners publish it, it is versioned, and agents look it up on demand.

How to handle a class of alert, how to write a release description, how to rebase the fork: each is a skill file, imported once and enabled per agent. Once, after the agent that checks the price list every day posted its report, a colleague asked in the thread whether it had failed to find the matching skill. It confirmed it had not found it and had instead done a read-only comparison following the channel's earlier practice. The colleague pointed out the skill was in the repository. The agent found it, confirmed its earlier search had skipped hidden directories by default, and recorded the problem in its working conventions for later runs.

An agent's workspace is a Git repository, so what one agent writes, any agent's next session can read; the daemons on colleagues' own machines use the same repositories. Tickets are maintained by the agent doing the work: updated as it progresses, closed when done, linked to the documents and pull requests.

The local Claude Code and Codex memory mechanism is unchanged; we only point the memory directory at a Git repository, with hooks that pull at session start and push at session end, so it is the same memory across machines and harnesses. The platform agents' memory is a separate system, layered by conversation source as described above. Merging the two is something we may do later.

Today's agents are triggered by a mention, a schedule, a pull request or issue event, a webhook, or a judgement (is this spam, which agent should take it), and they do the one thing the trigger specifies.

Proactive agents. Make the agent more like a teammate: it checks the things it is responsible for and proposes before acting. For example, it notices that a just-merged pull request changed behaviour and the matching design document was not updated, and proposes a documentation pull request in the development channel; or the same question has come up three times this week in a customer group, and it proposes writing the answer into the docs. It acts only once a person approves. This is in preview.

Computer use. Some things have no API, only a desktop app or a browser. Register a machine as a Computer, grant it to specific agents, and they can take screenshots, click and type; a person watches the live screen in the console and can take over at any time. Also in preview.

Joining meetings. Today a meeting is recorded, transcribed and processed afterwards by the documentation agent. Further out, the agent joins the meeting as a participant: it can be asked questions during the meeting, and decisions get written back to design documents and issues on the spot rather than afterwards. This has not started.

AgentConnect is open source under Apache 2.0 and deploys with Docker Compose or Kubernetes. If you hit a problem, open an issue; if you find it useful, you are welcome to star the repository.

https://github.com/agentconnect-md/agentconnect

Two Paths to the Future: The Paradigm Shift Facing Every Developer

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The current conversation surrounding AI and software engineering is dominated by anxiety. We debate when AI will replace us, why our workloads are increasing despite automation, and how the junior developer funnel is changing. However, these discussions often assume one fundamental thing: that our ultimate goal as developers must always be to *remain employees of traditional corporations. * If AI systematically shifts the job market, the lesson we must learn isn't to panic. Instead, we must realize that a major crossroads has emerged. Every technologist must now consciously choose which path to follow and understand the tradeoffs involved.

*Path 1: The Corporate Specialist (Labor Leverage) * This is the traditional route. In this ecosystem, you adapt to becoming a system governor, architect, and debugger-of-last-resort. You focus on managing complex legacy codebases, integrating advanced architectures like WebMCP, and guiding armies of autonomous agents to deliver enterprise objectives.

The advantage of this path is structure, scale, and access to massive corporate resources. However, the macroeconomic reality is hard to ignore. If major enterprises use autonomous agents to massively downsize their engineering departments, systemic employment drops.

This creates a dangerous economic loop: if disposable income vanishes across the workforce, who will buy the products these automated companies create? Relying solely on corporate hiring pipelines means tying your career security to an increasingly volatile and unpredictable corporate lifecycle.

*Path 2: The Sovereign Developer (AI Leverage) * This path represents a shift toward true ownership and self-reliance. Instead of studying solely to fit into a company's pre-existing architecture, you study to build independent systems.

The very same AI capabilities that allow a corporation to run with fewer people also give a single individual the production power of an entire traditional IT department. Historically, solo developers were severely bottlenecked by time and specialization. You could write great backend logic, but you might struggle with UI design, marketing, or cloud infrastructure.

Today, autonomous agents act as the ultimate force multipliers, seamlessly filling those execution gaps. On this path, your goal isn't to look for a seat at the corporate table. Instead, you use AI to build what those companies do, but directly for yourself, your clients, or your local community. You shift your focus from selling your labor to owning your product.

*The Decision Before Us * The future of technology is no longer just about learning the next popular framework or programming language; it is about choosing your leverage model.

Do you want to optimize your skills to remain indispensable to a legacy corporation? Or do you want to use the democratization of AI tools to build your own independent ecosystem? Both paths require deep technical mastery, critical thinking, and intense dedication, but they lead to entirely different destinations.

We don't need to stop working or stop learning. We just need to decide who we are building for. The tools are entirely in your hands, and the choice is yours. Which path are you choosing?

Major tech stocks plunge as US midterms add to fears of AI slowdown

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of billions of dollars in market value has been wiped from major tech firms in a stock market rout after the US midterm elections sparked concerns of a slowdown in AI development.

Shares in firms across the tech and artificial intelligence sectors plummeted following Democratic wins after election campaigns run on slowing AI.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq composite stock index closed down by more than 5.5% to lose an estimated $2.5tn (Â£1.9tn) in market capitalisation, according to Dow Jones data, while other key US stock indexes also saw big losses in their worst days since 2025.

The sell-off comes on top of broader concerns about whether the huge amounts being spent on AI will pay off.

Democrats in races across the US campaigned to slow the onslaught of data centres and AI, and there were dozens of measures on ballots around the country aimed at restricting the development of the hubs crucial for powering the technology.

They won both the US Senate and House of Representatives in Tuesday's midterms, taking control of Congress for the rest of US President Donald Trump's presidency.

Texas' Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned heavily against data centres, pledging a moratorium on new construction in the sector.

In a social media post, Trump blamed the slide on his political opponents.

"The Stock Market suffered one of the Worst Days in History based on the results of the Election," he said.

"Remember, it wasn't Republicans, it was Dumocrats. Do not fear, however, we will soon, one way or the other, be BIGGER AND STRONGER THAN EVER BEFORE. MAKE AMERICA GREAT AGAIN!"

Some Democrats have argued that AI has grown too big without any safeguards or oversight and have promised, with their new power in Congress, to change that.

"Americans don't want Big Tech oligarchs wrecking the environment, gutting jobs and threatening humanity's future. NATIONAL DATA CENTER MORATORIUM NOW," Democrat Senator Bernie Sanders said in a post on X on Thursday.

Shares in companies heavily exposed to AI data centres, including crucial chipmakers and semiconductor manufacturers, fell sharply on Thursday, highlighting investor concerns that had already been growing.

Nvidia shares slid more than 9%, knocking more than $400bn off the firm's market value.

Other chipmakers also suffered, with AMD and Micron both dropping by more than 17%. Intel shares also dropped, down 10.3%, while memory card maker SanDisk plunged more than 22%.

The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks the performance of 30 of the largest companies involved in semiconductor production, fell by more than 12%.

Shares in Elon Musk's companies SpaceX and Tesla also plunged, falling 8% and nearly 11% respectively and shaving more than $300bn off the firms' market value on Thursday.

Musk had spent nearly $450m backing Republican campaigns, including at least $45m in support of Ken Paxton for the Texas senate seat, but Texas was turned blue in the Democratic tsunami on Tuesday night.

In total, the so-called magnificent seven tech companies, including Apple, Nvidia and Tesla, have shed a trillion dollars in value in a single trading day.

The stock falls also hit other major US stock indexes. The S&P 500 index fell by 4.4% by the end of trading, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average erased gains made on Wednesday to close down more than 3.4%.

The Nasdaq Composite fall was so large it is now in what market watchers call correction territory, which is where an index or company value falls at least 10% below its recent high.

Boebert, billionaires' tax and other midterms races still to watch

Susannah Streeter, chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said the steep sell-off on the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 was sending shockwaves into other markets.

"Fresh panic over the AI trade has hit Wall Street hard," she said.

"Fears that the vast sums poured into AI infrastructure may not deliver the returns investors have been banking on are now colliding with the prospect of a sharp slowdown in data centre construction."

Some tech leaders have said they were caught off guard by the backlash against data centres in the midterms.

OpenAI president and co-founder Greg Brockman said he believed everyone had been surprised by the reaction to data centres, and more work needed to be done to ensure communities were on board.

"A lot of our shift over the course of this year has really been to realise that from San Francisco trying to build infrastructure in Texas, Georgia, and other places, you can't just tweet and say: 'This is going to be good.' You need to be in those communities," Brockman said at the WSJ Tech conference in Napa, California on Wednesday evening.

Meta's president Dina Powell McCormick said that the industry needed to put more effort into selling AI as a good idea.

"I think we need to double and triple down on telling the story," she told the WSJ conference.

Alongside the immediate impacts of the US mid-term elections, some investors have become concerned that AI shares could be in a bubble that is due a correction.

Gate boasts 104% cold reserve coverage, but separate snapshot dates raise red flags

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Its new hot-wallet loss test uses separate measurement bases, while independent verification of current PoSEC data remains in progress.

Crypto exchange Gate has introduced Proof of Security, a disclosure framework that proves its cold assets cover customer balances if it lost all its hot-wallet assets.

Its Nov. 5 announcement reports 104.58% cold-storage coverage, using a storage snapshot taken at 00:00 UTC on Nov. 4.

The published percentage compares asset shares from different snapshots, rather than establishing cold assets and customer balances on a single common date. Gate discloses that limitation, so customers need more than the headline ratio to verify the full-backing claim.

Gate's PoSEC methodology compares an 88.26% cold-storage share with an 84.39% user-asset share. The remaining shares are 11.74% in hot wallets and 15.61% owned by the platform.

Cold storage is measured against cold assets plus hot-wallet assets in the storage snapshot, while user assets are measured against platform reserves in the separate Proof of Reserves snapshot. The FAQ defines the coverage calculation as the first share divided by the second.

The page explicitly says the snapshots have different totals and that intervening price changes and fund movements are not reflected.

Same-date backing needs aligned balances. Gate’s reported percentage combines shares from different snapshot totals.

The reported 104.58% share comparison alone cannot establish backing at one common date.

Different snapshot dates and totals; intervening price changes and fund movements are excluded. The earlier PoR reference is not separately dated in the chart. This does not establish insolvency or losses.

Rings normalize each reported share to its own 100% total; they do not compare absolute dollar values. Arc lengths use the displayed percentages. 88.26+11.74=100 and 84.39+15.61=100. The 104.58% result is Gate-reported, not recomputed from rounded display inputs.

Different snapshot dates and totals; intervening price changes and fund movements are excluded. The earlier PoR reference is not separately dated in the chart. This does not establish insolvency or losses.

Gate's Oct. 21 disclosure reports roughly $8.7 billion in reserves and $7.3 billion in user net assets as of Oct. 18, with a 118.49% overall reserve ratio.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The PoSEC chart does not separately date its earlier user-asset reference, and the October dollar totals cannot be combined with November storage percentages to present a measured dollar buffer.

A direct test of backing after a hot-wallet loss would compare surviving cold assets with customer balances at the same time, using compatible valuations and the same reporting perimeter.

The disclosure leaves a gap in demonstrating common-date coverage, as it doesn't establish either insolvency or customer losses.

Gate's definition includes digital assets in cold wallets and traditional assets, such as stocks and US Treasuries, held by independent custodians.

The exchange says qualifying assets must sit outside routine deposit and withdrawal operations, be offline or independently custodied, require multiparty transfer authorization, and be verifiable. It also explains that off-chain traditional assets can make reported cold storage exceed balances visible in blockchain cold wallets.

As of Nov. 5, the PoSEC page lists independent third-party verification as in progress. Gate says the review covers asset authenticity and control, valuation and reporting scope, and the coverage calculation.

Gate's PoSEC FAQ says individual wallet addresses are currently withheld for security reasons, making the promised independent report particularly relevant to public scrutiny.

That review should be distinguished from the older audit shown on Gate's Proof of Reserves page. The page dates a Hacken implementation assessment to Jan. 3, 2024. Hacken describes that work as examining code, architecture, build processes, and vulnerabilities.

The new framework gives customers a concrete scenario to evaluate. The decisive next evidence is a common-date reconciliation of cold assets and customer balances, with valuation methods, entity scope, custody restrictions, and asset composition clear enough to assess the claim.

A completed review can show how those pieces fit together, while a comparison of percentages from different snapshots cannot do that by itself.

Apple expected to bid for F1 streaming rights in more countries, says Formula 1 CEO

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On the latest earnings call for F1’s parent company Liberty Media, the CEO of Formula 1 Stefano Domenicali said that both parties are happy with the US deal, and he expects that Apple will bid for rights in more countries as the opportunities become available.

F1 has previously expressed that they are not currently looking for one media partner to take global broadcast rights for the sport; they want a portfolio of deals, picking the best partners for each geographic market.

We have already seen this play out in the United Kingdom, with broadcaster Sky renewing its deal for F1 rights through 2034, seemingly locking Apple out of that region. So, despite the apparent demand, Apple will not be able to just walk in and take over the future of the sport’s worldwide distribution.

However, it seems the league is open to striking more deals with Apple in certain markets.

On the earnings call, in response to a question from an investor about Apple’s interest, Domenicali said “this is part of our discussion, if there are opportunities that can be in other markets, they will do it … they’re going to do it as soon as these opportunities will be open for them”.

Liberty Media CEO Derek Chang said they continue to think about what new innovation they can bring to the product side, and noted that “Apple as a potential bidder in other markets is helpful” for their business.

As a reminder, all Formula 1 races are available to watch live and on-demand with an Apple TV subscription, in the United States. The company recently launched a Dolby Atmos audio track for its F1 streams, as well as expanded support for Multiview on more platforms, including iPhone Duo.

Five9 buys contact center intelligence startup Level AI after posting narrow earnings beat

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Contact center company Five9 Inc. flexed its acquisitive muscles today as it announced a $325 million all-cash deal to buy an artificial intelligence-native rival called Level AI as it posted its third-quarter financial results. The deal is expected to close in the fourth quarter, subject to customary closing conditions, Five9 said.

Five9 is a leading player in the cloud contact center software industry, selling tools and services that enterprises use to manage their customer service interactions. Its web-based software spans traditional phone calls, video calls, emails, messaging, social media and other channels.

As for Level AI, it’s the developer of a contact center and customer experience intelligence platform that uses artificial intelligence to support human agents on calls and analyze, score and augment their interactions with the callers they serve. Its platform is designed to listen into customer service agent’s calls, so it can provide real-time guidance throughout and support workflow automation. The insights it generates can also be used to assist in agent coaching.

Level AI also develops autonomous AI agents that can automate some simpler customer inquiries and background operational tasks, freeing up human agents to focus on more complex customer issues. “Level AI’s AI applications help organizations improve agent performance, automate more customer interactions across voice and digital channels, and turn conversations into insights the whole organization can use,” Five9 said in a blog post announcing the deal. “Its applications are in production with enterprise customers today and handle more than one billion customer interactions a year.”

Five9 said it intends to continue offering Level AI’s applications as standalone tools for organizations that don’t want to shift their on-premises contact center operations to the cloud, also those who are using other cloud contact center platforms. However, it also plans to integrate the startup’s tools with its Genius CX Platform. “As AI reshapes the customer experience landscape, we believe adding Level AI strengthens our competitive position, expands the customers we can serve, and deepens the AI talent behind our continuous innovation,” said Five9 chief executive officer Amit Mathradas.

Level AI founder and CEO Ashish Nagar said that by joining Five9, his company will be able to bring its applications and customer service-native AI models to thousands of new contact centers around the world, while allowing it to continue supporting its existing customers.

Investors don’t seem to have shared the CEOs’ enthusiasm for the deal, however, for Five9’s stock fell more than 23% in late trading after the deal was announced. That may be because of the cost of the acquisition. The $325 million cash outlay amounts to almost half of Five9’s current cash and marketable investments, which stood at $738 million at the end of the third quarter.

Five9’s results weren’t too bad. The company reported adjusted earnings of 82 cents per share on revenue of $323.3 million, surpassing Wall Street’s guidance of 80 cents per share in earnings and $318.3 million in sales. But it’s notable that the company’s full-year guidance excludes any contributions from the pending deal, and so its value hinges on Five9’s ability to rapidly integrate Level AI’s technology.

For the current quarter, Five9 said it’s expecting earnings of between 88 cents and 92 cents per share on sales of $328 million to $334 million. But that guidance was mixed. Wall Street is looking for significantly higher earnings of 99 cents per share and $330.7 million in revenue.

For the full year, Five9 said it’s looking for earnings of $3.17 to $3.21 per share on total sales of $1.27 billion. Wall Street wants to see higher earnings of $3.26 per share on similar revenue.

Is Appian a Buy After Its Latest Earnings Report?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Appian's AI momentum accelerated in the third quarter.

Appian (APPN +8.45%) came into its third-quarter earnings report in the midst of its strongest rally since the pandemic, up more than 150% from its lows this year.

The process automation software specialist has seen its growth and profitability ramp up in the AI era as the company has incorporated AI across its software platform, and Appian AI, its AI-powered app-building software, is seeing explosive growth.

Customer AI usage jumped 50% sequentially in the third quarter, showing its base of government and enterprise customers are finding value in the company's AI tools. For example, Appian AI helped customers conduct audits three times faster, perform underwriting reviews 75% faster, and run investment processes with 34% greater efficiency.

As a result of its AI growth, overall revenue jumped 25% to 234.6 million, well ahead of the consensus at $217 million. Revenue growth benefited in part from a surge in on-premises revenue, driven by the busiest quarter in the public sector. Cloud subscription revenue, which Appian generally considers its core focus, was up 20% in the quarter to $136.4 million, and total subscription revenue was up 28% to $187.8 million.

On the bottom line, adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) rose from $32.2 million to $43.4 million, and adjusted earnings per share jumped from $0.32 to $0.51, well ahead of the consensus at $0.33.

Appian's guidance called for its momentum to continue into the fourth quarter with cloud subscription revenue guidance of $143 million-$145 million, representing 22%-24% growth, and total revenue guidance of $233 million-$237 million, representing growth of 15%-17%. On the bottom line, it sees adjusted earnings per share of $0.30-$0.34, up from $0.15 in the quarter a year ago. For the full year, adjusted earnings per share are expected to double to $1.20-$1.24.

Appian stock was trading up 4% Thursday afternoon after jumping nearly 20% in the premarket session, though the rout in the broader stock market erased some of those gains.

Only a few years ago, Appian was struggling in the post-pandemic era, and as recently as 2024, the company was losing money on an EBITDA basis.

However, Appian has reinvented itself, streamlining its sales force and reorganizing its go-to-market strategy to be more efficient, and those efforts paid off in improved profitability.

In the AI era, the company also sees itself as having established a competitive advantage through its years of work with process automation before AI and its established trust with organizations like government agencies and multinational banks, which have mission-critical applications and prioritize reliability.

Speaking in an interview with The Motley Fool, Appian CEO Matt Calkins commented that its competition has actually decreased in the AI era because the company is now competing at a higher tier. He explained, saying, "The name of the game is reliability because we're talking at the top of the organization and above all they want AI connected to their most important work in a reliable way."

Calkins also said that Appian had done a good job of predicting the evolution of the process automation market, saying that AI had changed the market it's competing in, and added, "I think we've done a number of things right in the last few years, but what I think in retrospect we've done is to see the obvious a little bit in advance."

Above all, Appian is meeting the moment by providing a comprehensive platform built on its data fabric, a top-rated orchestration layer, governance protocols to ensure safety, and an efficiency layer that ensures tokens are used in a cost-effective manner.

On the earnings call, CFO Serge Tanjga said, "We all show up here for work knowing that we should be a much bigger company in a few years," showing strong confidence in the business’s direction.

Appian has the momentum, and AI is providing a massive opportunity. The company expects margins to continue to expand as it grows, meaning 2027 should be another year of strong profit growth. Given that, Appian stock looks like a good bet to keep moving higher.

I Can’t Imagine Getting Into A Brawl At Disney World, But It Happened (& Now There’s A Lawsuit)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I've Been Counting Down To DWTS's Disney Night, But It's Leaving Something Out That Upsets Me

What happened to Disney theme parks being the happiest place on Earth?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Disney theme parks are supposed to be the happiest place on earth, where you spend time making memories with family and loved ones. That is, until someone gets into an all-out brawl in the line for Avatar Flight of Passage in Pandora – The World of Avatar inside Disney’s Animal Kingdom, and it results in a lawsuit. Apparently, the individuals involved had been arguing for the entirety of their 80-minute wait, and it finally reached a boiling point, leading to several people seemingly getting physical.

While I find it wild that this happened at all, what makes it even wilder is the fact that this is not the first time a brawl has taken place in a ride queue. Line cutting at Indiana Jones Adventure at Disneyland led to an incident when the person who was doing the cutting hit a man who said something about it, which incited a brawl and resulted in the attacked man sustaining a minor injury.

Pertaining to the fight in Pandora, a series of Reddit posts point to an unknown cause for the arguing between two groups, stating that it didn’t appear to have anything to do with cutting in line. Honestly, regardless of what sparked the fighting, that kind of behavior can’t and shouldn’t be tolerated, and apparently Morgan & Morgan feels the same way, as they’re suing Disney on behalf of a family that was struck several times.

In a statement to Florida Politics, Morgan & Morgan's founder John Morgan and attorney Christopher Ryan explained what reportedly happened to their clients. They also stated the family's expectation for a safe trip at Disney World:

Our clients were on a family vacation at Disney’s Animal Kingdom when they were confronted by a group of adults in line for Avatar Flight of Passage. The encounter allegedly escalated into a physical altercation in which our clients, including their teenage daughter, were struck several times. Families come to Disney expecting to be safe.

In the complaint, it's explained that this group "allegedly pushed Plaintiff and her family, climbed over queue barriers, and struck Plaintiff." It also states that the plaintiff sustained "bodily injuries and damages."

The lawsuit also accused employees of not monitoring the queue, failing to perform adequate crowd control, and not properly de-escalating the situation.

Millions of people visit Disney theme parks every year, and even though these stories of bad behavior tend to make headlines, it does involve a relatively very small number of individuals in comparison. That’s not to say any instance of violence is acceptable (it’s very much not okay). Whether it’s two families throwing fists at Magic Kingdom or a fistfight in Mickey’s Toontown at Disneyland, or even that scuffle over a Disney World photo op, this should just not be happening in any capacity.

In the past, viral videos of Disney World skirmishes tend to get a lot of attention. Lifetime bans from Disney Parks can be lifted, but it requires those banned to send letters of apology to request that the ban be lifted, and it’s very rare that the decision gets reversed.

Sometimes, lawsuits happen too, which is what it has come to after the brawl that broke out on Flight of Passage.

Lionsgate & 3 Arts Set New Horror Banner Render Road Led By Luke Maxwell & Will Rowbotham – The Dish

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Halloween may be over, but the horrors never cease — election notwithstanding. With that, some big news today: Sources tell Deadline that Lionsgate and subsidiary 3 Arts Entertainment are partnering to launch Render Road, a new horror production banner.

This is the initiative Lionsgate CEO Jon Feltheimer alluded to on today’s Q2 earnings call, when he referred to something to come “soon” from 3 Arts in the horror space. Luke Maxwell and Will Rowbotham, two of the manager-producers leading the charge in cultivating new generations of horror filmmaking talent, will spearhead the effort for 3 Arts.

Render Road, we’re told, will develop, produce and finance projects for digital alongside traditional films and television series. This comes as those two lanes continue to converge. The company will collaborate with up-and-comers and established artists alike; they’’l shepherd some projects independently, alongside these artists, and guide others in partnership with other studios and production companies. Watch on Deadline

RELATED: Top 50 Halloween Movies Photo Gallery: Creepy Classics From ‘Psycho,’ ‘The Shining’ To ‘Scream’ & ‘Get Out’

The new banner allows Lionsgate to benefit more directly from 3 Arts’ talent pipeline. Serving in a sense as curators, 3 Arts’ Maxwell and Rowbotham will in turn build on Lionsgate’s legacy as home to such major horror properties as Saw, Blair Witch and House of 1000 Corpses.

To be clear, Maxwell and Rowbotham will remain managers at 3 Arts while at the helm of Render Road, which will be open to all, rather than collaborating exclusively with 3 Arts talent. Meanwhile, it’s understood that Maxwell and Rowbotham will take their clients’ lead on the destination for their own projects.

3 Arts — and the Render Road duo of Maxwell and Rowbotham, in particular — have made major waves in horror in recent years, demonstrating a savvy in identifying the star filmmakers of tomorrow and bringing them through their breakout projects.

RELATED: Ultimate Final Girls In Horror: From Laurie Strode To Sidney Prescott & Dorothy Gale

Most notably of late, client Kane Parsons smashed records this summer with over $400M for horror phenomenon Backrooms, the highest grossing film in A24 history. Alongside Curry Barker’s Obsession ($523MWW), the film is one of the biggest of the year in the genre and has brought creator-driven horror to the town’s attention as the next big thing in theatrical. This, as Gen Z and millennials lead the way in theatrical attendance growth, per a recent Fandango study. Outgrossing The Mandalorian and Grogu, Backrooms set A24 opening records in 41 countries; Parsons was just 19 years old when we exclusively reported that he’d been signed to direct the film, based on his same-name web series and the creepypasta that inspired it.

Beyond Parsons, 3 Arts reps such buzzy talents as Ian Tuason, of A24’s Undertone, who is directing the next installment of the Paranormal Activity franchise, and Nic Curcio, who made Play House for little more than 100K after winning a Fantastic Fest pitch competition and a year later wound up with distribution through Focus Features. Then, there’s Dylan Clark, who is directing Lionsgate and Blumhouse Atomic’s new Blair Witch as well as Lionsgate’s Portrait of God. The latter time, starring One Battle After Another’s Chase Infiniti, is bringing horror icons Sam Raimi and Jordan Peele together as producers for the first time.

RELATED: 17 Witch Movies From ‘Hocus Pocus’ To ‘Wizard Of Oz’ And Where They Are Streaming

As mentioned, Parsons and Curry’s success has led the industry toward something of a fundamental paradigm shift — at the very least, a reassessment of the kinds of projects that warrant greenlighting and who to make them with. The duo’s success followed that of Markiplier, who with the self-financed and self-distributed video game adaptation Iron Lung ($50MWW), took the first shot across the bow to demonstrate that creators will play a substantial role in the future of Hollywood, and entertainment, more broadly.

The summer of Obsession and Backrooms has led studios down the path of investing further in young digital native filmmakers, and quickly. Focus Features set up Barker’s follow-up Anything but Ghosts as part of the deal to release Obsession. Meanwhile, studios have gotten behind young talent on horror titles such as The Mandela Catalogue (Amazon/UA/Amblin), Siren Head (Warner Bros.), Cartoon Cat (TriStar) and Portrait of God (Lionsgate) — some based on preexisting IP, some not.

RELATED: Vampire Movies And Where They Are Streaming: From ‘Twilight’ To ‘Bram Stoker’s Dracula’

Most recently kicking off production on a new chapter of Blair Witch and greenlighting Portrait of God, Lionsgate is presently at work developing the next installment of Saw, directed by Mike P. Nelson, as well as Sam Raimi’s remake of the ventriloquist dummy horror flick Magic.

News of Render Road comes amid a shifting landscape in studio horror, with the introduction of Paramount Primal, and Focus Features’ apparent decision to go all in on the space.

Core Scientific to quit Bitcoin mining by year-end as AI business surges

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The former Bitcoin mining heavyweight plans to become a pure-play data center provider in 2027, with mining now accounting for just 7.5% of quarterly revenue.

Core Scientific said it expects to exit Bitcoin mining by the end of 2026 as it shifts its business toward artificial intelligence and high-performance computing infrastructure.

“We expect to complete the wind-down during the fourth quarter and enter 2027 as a pure-play, high-density colocation provider,” said Core Scientific chief financial officer Jim Nygaard during a Q3 earnings call on Thursday.

Core Scientific had previously disclosed in a July quarterly filing that hosted mining would end by Dec. 31 as it continued to wind down its self-mining operations. The comment establishes a targeted completion date for its complete Bitcoin mining exit.

Its latest quarterly results show how far the business has shifted. Third-quarter self-mining revenue fell to $12.3 million from $57.4 million a year earlier, while revenue from hosting other miners dropped to $5.2 million from $8.7 million.

Bitcoin mining generated just $17.5 million, or 7.5%, of Core Scientific’s $232.9 million in third-quarter revenue, compared with $215.4 million from colocation services.

The company had nearly 50% fewer miners online at the end of September than at the end of 2025, according to Nygaard.

Core Scientific’s move into AI hosting took shape in 2024 after signing an initial hosting agreement with CoreWeave in March that year, followed by a much larger deal announced in June.

At the time, Core Scientific presented AI hosting as an expansion alongside its mining business. Its June 2024 announcement emphasized preserving Bitcoin mining capacity.

By December 2025, The Wall Street Journal reported that Core Scientific was already targeting to wind down its Bitcoin mining operations by 2028.

Related: American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

Mining economics added pressure in the months following.

Core Scientific recorded $266.5 million in mining-related asset impairments in the first quarter of 2026, citing weaker Bitcoin prices, historically low hashprice and falling digital asset mining equipment values in the secondary market.

During the second quarter, it terminated its mining equipment agreement with Block and Proto, recording a $41.9 million loss as part of the transition.

The planned exit comes as the US election results introduce fresh uncertainty for companies expanding AI infrastructure.

Opponents of data-center development were among the winners of the Democrats’ sweeping midterm gains, Reuters reported Wednesday.

The backlash centers partly on electricity bills and the demands large facilities place on local resources.

Cointelegraph reached out to Core Scientific for comment.

YouTube’s biggest creators join forces to support the Susan Wojcicki Foundation’s critical mission: changing the conversation about lung cancer

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In August 2024, YouTube‘s former longtime CEO, Susan Wojcicki, passed away at age 56. She battled non-small-cell lung cancer for two years and left behind one heck of a legacy.

Now Susan’s husband Dennis Troper and her sisters, Dr. Janet Wojcicki and Anne Wojcicki, have teamed up with professionals in clinical research, AI, diagnostics, and early detection to launch the Susan Wojcicki Foundation, which wants to “change the conversation” around lung cancer and help more people access screening, early detection, and prevention.

And, fittingly, the SWF is marking Lung Cancer Awareness Month by telling people about its mission on YouTube–with the help of creators.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

MrBeast, Mark Rober, Hank Green, Lilly Singh, Cassey Ho, Marina Mogilko, Cleo Abram, Mark Vins, Jake Roper, sWooZie, Mat & Stephanie Patrick, Dr. Mike, Stephanie Chuang, and Rhett & Link all joined the SWF’s first YouTube video, offering their advice about how it can most effectively connect with viewers and share its message.

“The channel and its contents will address the fact that anyone with lungs can get lung cancer, and nobody deserves it,” Audrey Eatherly, the SWF’s Head of Creator and Community Partnerships, tells Tubefilter. “Early detection and prevention are key. But stigma and confusion keep too many people from talking about it or asking about a screening.”

The widespread assumption that only smokers can get lung cancer and the societal propensity to blame people who get it is a critical challenge to helping them, she adds. Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer deaths worldwide, killing more people each year than leukemia, lymphoma, and breast and prostate cancers combined.

“And yet, it receives the least amount of funding per death,” she says. “The stigma and misunderstanding have been barriers to addressing this critical challenge.”

Yvonne Diaz, a co-founder & Chair @ Oncogene Cancer Research and Board Member @ ALK Positive who has stage 4 ALK+ lung cancer, puts it well: “No matter how you wound up with lung cancer, you deserve the best outcomes, the best care, the best support, and empathy.”

Eatherly says the SWF wanted to bring its mission home to YouTube because the platform has the power to “transform lung cancer research into meaningful human conversations, bringing experts, research, real stories, shared experiences, and understanding to the forefront.”

The channel will share “credible, science-backed information and recent advancements” through things like deep-dive interviews with experts, informative Shorts, FAQs, and creator collaborations, she says.

Speaking of creators, they aren’t just helping launch the SWF’s YouTube channel. Jake Roper, Mark Vins (aka Brave Wilderness), sWooZie, and Mat & Stephanie Patrick are all on the foundation’s official Creator Advisory Council.

“As longtime creators on YouTube, Matthew and I recognize the massive impact the platform has had on the world and how Susan Wojcicki’s leadership shaped its growth for almost a decade,” the Patricks tell Tubefilter. “The Susan Wojcicki Foundation carries Susan’s legacy forward through the very platform she helped build, and the creators whose careers she made possible are here to help spread its message. While YouTube is an entertainment platform, for many people it’s also their primary source of information and education online, making it a powerful place for the Susan Wojcicki Foundation to share a global message about lung cancer awareness and treatment.”

Stephanie also notes that she has a master’s degree in cancer pharmacology, and has seen firsthand “the massive scientific advancements made in the last decade in cancers that were previously thought to be untreatable.”

“The research the Susan Wojcicki Foundation is supporting is incredibly promising and could lead to significant improvements in patient outcomes while accelerating progress in lung cancer care,” she says.

Vins adds that he’s dedicated to the SWF because he wants to honor Susan’s lasting presence in the creator space.

“Susan was revered as a business leader who helped shape the technology and media landscape, but to creators, she was revered for something even more personal—her unwavering belief in our potential,” he says. “She empowered our voices at a time when the possibilities of YouTube were only beginning to emerge. She understood that creators weren’t simply making videos, we were building communities and creating relationships that would make a meaningful impact in the world.

“Ultimately, the greatest way I can honor Susan’s legacy is to do exactly what she always encouraged us to do: speak from the heart, create with integrity, and use our platforms to make a positive difference. Susan believed in the power of creators to change the world. I hope we can continue proving her right to bring greater awareness to lung cancer and support the pursuit of early detection that will save lives.”

You can check out the SWF’s YouTube channel here, and read more about its mission on its website.

Blood banks and grocery stores across Europe hit by cyberattacks

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cyberattacks targeted several European countries this week, including grocery stores across the UK and the European Union, as well as three cold storage facilities in France that house some of the nation's blood supply.

The blood facilities were targeted on Sunday and Monday, with the temperature of the remotely controlled containers raised to "very high levels," according to EFS, France's public blood, plasma, and platelets service. Technicians were alerted to the attack by alarms and moved the blood before the supplies were rendered unusable. Not long after, supermarket freezers and refrigerators in the UK, Italy, Germany, Romania, Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, Holland, and Sweden reportedly malfunctioned, with some machines going into defrost mode. Temperatures in some of the cooling units soared to 122 degrees Fahrenheit, causing millions of euros in spoiled food, the Guardian reported.

The grocery store malfunctions were reminiscent of cyber-enabled attacks that hit hundreds of French grocery stores last month, which not only ruined food but sparked fires. Similarly, in August, commissaries at half a dozen US military bases reported that their freezers were remotely set to defrost overnight, ruining food supplies. An investigation into the incidents by the Defense Department is ongoing.

No individual or group has taken responsibility for the blood and grocery store incidents this week or earlier this year. Intelligence experts in Europe have posited that Russian officials are possibly recruiting people to commit sabotage in NATO nations.
