Axcelis (ACLS) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bookings hit their highest level since late 2023 as chip equipment demand rebounds.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Axcelis Technologies, Inc. (ACLS -5.03%) reported third quarter results that surpassed internal expectations for revenue and earnings, supported by record revenue in the Customer Solutions and Innovation segment and a significant increase in total bookings. Management reported that demand is recovering across power, memory, and general mature nodes, with particularly high engagement in silicon carbide applications for electric vehicles and data centers. The company is expanding its manufacturing footprint in South Korea to support long-term capacity requirements while progressing toward its planned merger with Veeco Instruments. Management indicated that the company enters 2027 with positive momentum and expects growth across all primary end markets based on rising customer utilization rates and a strengthening book-to-bill ratio.

Operator: Good day, ladies and gentlemen, and welcome to the Axcelis Technologies call to discuss the company's results for the third quarter of 2026. My name is Rivka, and I will be your coordinator for today. I would now like to turn the presentation over to your host for today's call, David Ryzhik, senior vice president and interim chief financial officer. Please go ahead.

David Ryzhik: Thank you, operator. This is David Ryzhik, senior vice president and interim chief financial officer with me today is Russell J. Low, president and CEO. If you have not seen a copy of our press release issued earlier today, it is available on our website. In addition, we have prepared slides accompanying today's call and you can find those on our website as well. Playback service will also be available on our website as described in our press release. Please note that comments made today about our expectations for future revenues profits and other results are forward looking statements under the SEC's Safe Harbor provision.

These forward looking statements are based on management's current expectations and are subject to the risks inherent in our business. These risks are described in detail in our annual report on Form 10 k and other SEC filings, which we urge you to review. Our actual results may differ materially from our current expectations. We do not assume any obligation to update these forward looking statements. Given the pending merger with Veco, we will not be addressing questions related to the transaction. During this call, we will be discussing various non GAAP financial measures. Unless otherwise noted, all income statement related financial measures will be non GAAP other than revenue.

Please refer to our press release and accompanying materials for information regarding our non GAAP financial results and a reconciliation to our GAAP. Now I will turn the call over to President and CEO, Russell J. Low.

Russell J. Low: Thank you, David. Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us for our third quarter 2026 earnings call.

David Ryzhik: Before I get into our quarterly results today, I would like to share a brief leadership update. As we announced on October 27, we are pleased to welcome Yaron Delsman as executive vice president and chief financial officer beginning November 12. Yaron brings deep financial expertise, extensive global semiconductor industry experience a strategic mindset, and proven leadership ability to the role. In a demonstrated track record of leading organizations through phases of growth and transformation, building strong teams, and translating strategy into measurable results. I look forward to partnering with him as we pursue our growth opportunities and build an even stronger company through our next chapter.

I would also like to thank David for his contributions and leadership while serving as interim CFO. Following Yaron's arrival, David will transition to his new role as senior vice president corporate development, and investor relations, reporting directly to me. His deep knowledge of Axcelis, our markets, and our business will be especially valuable as we continue to advance our strategic priorities.

Russell J. Low: Turning to our performance in the quarter, we continue to build momentum in the third quarter as the demand backdrop strengthened further. Reported revenue of $238 million and earnings per diluted share of $1.26, which exceeded our expectations due to higher revenue and lower operating expenses more than offsetting lower gross margins due to foreign-exchange headwinds. These results reflect sequential improvement in systems delivered combined with another quarterly record in CS&I revenue. As expected, systems growth in the quarter was driven by an improvement in power and memory markets, partially offset by a relatively small sequential decline in general mature following a strong second quarter.

CS&I continues to exhibit strong momentum supported by a growing installed base higher customer utilization of our tools in the field, and the continued growth in our service contract and upgrade offerings. CS&I continues to represent an important driver of our long term growth and profitability across market cycles. System bookings grew significantly quarter over quarter with our book to bill coming in at 1.5. This further supports our view that the end markets we serve are strengthening that we are entering 2027 with positive momentum. Turning to our systems revenue by segment, sales to mature node applications accounted for approximately 78% of system shipments with memory and advanced logic making up the balance of our sales.

Now let me review our trends by end market. Beginning with power, sales from silicon carbide applications grew notably on a sequential basis. This reflects broad based improvements in demand, including custom pull ins in Europe and Asia. Combined with high utilization rates and increased customer engagement, this reinforces our view of a strengthening demand environment. We are also seeing an expanding range of applications driving silicon carbide adoption beyond additional automotive, industrial and solar markets. AI driven power requirements and data center investment are emerging as additional demand drivers. At the same time, declining wafer costs are improving the economics of silicon carbide across a broader range of applications including consumer and commercial products.

As a result of the investments we have made over the past few years in our technology, we are now well positioned to capitalize on these dynamics and the increasing adoption of more advanced device architectures and customers transition to 200 millimeter silicon carbide wafers. Our wafer handling technology enables demanding high temperature implant applications while ongoing and improvements in cost of ownership deliver value to customers and expand opportunities for our CS&I business. We are also beginning to see the benefits of our early investments in the precision and process control capabilities required to enable low cost superjunction adoption.

This is translating into growing commercial track with multiple tier 1 customers selecting our pure and family equipped with advanced channeling technology. Our other power markets, third quarter sales declined modestly or in a strong second quarter, but remained elevated compared to the prior year run rate. Demand continues to benefit from improving utilization across automotive, industrial, consumer, and data center applications. In silicon IGBT specifically, we are also seeing growing interest in our new proton implant solution with multiple orders for the pure VXE LT plus. Proton implantation is increasingly important in high performance silicon IGBTs, helping improve power efficiency and device reliability.

Also seeing growing interest in our solutions for power management ICs or PMICs supported in part by increasing data center demands. In general mature, sales declined modestly on a sequential basis, but remained consistent with levels seen throughout 2026. More importantly, bookings show strong growth on a sequential basis, following several years of softer demand supported by rising utilization rates and renewed customer investment. North America was a particularly strong contributor, reflecting increased customer investment, including capacity expansion plans associated with AI related demand.

General mature continues to represent a large market opportunity for Axcelis given the high implant intensity of these applications, our ability to address a wide range of customer requirements with our broad portfolio of high energy, high current, and medium current platforms. Our recently introduced Purion H6 high current system further strengthens our competitive position in this market, We are seeing increased customer interest as investment activity recovers. Turning to our advanced logic market, we shipped a follow on system we highlighted last quarter from materials modification application supporting 2 nanometer production.

We continue to work closely with leading customers on advanced node technology road maps where 2 nanometers and future process technologies are creating additional opportunities for material modification applications. In memory, the industry demand environment remains robust, supported by accelerating investments in DRAM and HBM capacity to address growing AI related requirements. We are seeing momentum in the engagements with our memory customers in Korea as well as with our customers outside of Korea. In fact, building on a strong presence in Korea, we recently celebrated the groundbreaking of a new manufacturing facility in Pyeongdaek, representing a $35 million investment in our global production capabilities.

We are especially pleased to mark the occasion alongside record representatives from our major Korean customers underscoring the strong relationships we have built in the region and our long term commitment to supporting their growth. The approximately 200 thousand square foot facility will consolidate and expand our Korean manufacturing operations and enhance our ability to respond quickly and with more flexibility to meet customer requirements. Production is expected to begin in the second half of 28, This investment represents an important step in expanding our global operations infrastructure to support our customers' long term growth plans. Now let me wrap up my thoughts and provide our perspective as we exit 2026 and move into 2027.

While the broader demand environment is clearly improving, we are particularly pleased to see us strategy of continuing to invest through the recent digestion period beginning to yield results. Our next generation technologies, including our Purion H6 high current implant are for general mature and new high energy proton implants solution for silicon IGBTs our advanced channeling technology for silicon carbide, and several other technologies are gaining traction as customers investment recovers.

In addition, we have seen increased interest in our highest energy solution, the Purion XEmax, and we also recently expanded our engagement with a leading European research institution with the placement of a medium current system to complement our existing high current system already installed, expanding our portfolio there. As we look ahead, we are increasingly encouraged by the broadening recovery across our end markets, and expect growth in 2027 across memory, power, and general mature. This outlook is supported by continued investment in DRAM and HBM capacity recovering conditions in carbide and growing activity across general mature applications. Higher customer utilization, increased bookings, and expanding investment plans further reinforce our confidence in the demand environment.

At the same time, our CSI business continues to provide a growing foundation for revenue and profitability. REGENXBIO (RGNX) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Royalty declines and a litigation gain shaped the quarter's results.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

REGENXBIO Inc. (RGNX -4.67%) is advancing toward a transition from a clinical-stage biotechnology firm to a commercial entity, with multiple regulatory milestones anticipated through 2027. Management is prioritizing the development of its lead gene therapy candidates, sura-vec for retinal diseases and RGX-202 for Duchenne muscular dystrophy, supported by a cash runway extending into late 2027. The company is leveraging its collaboration with AbbVie to advance sura-vec through pivotal trials while preparing a Biologics License Application for RGX-202 under the accelerated approval pathway. Financial performance in the third quarter was influenced by the expiration of certain royalty-bearing patents and a significant litigation settlement that strengthened the balance sheet.

Operator: Welcome, everyone, to the Third Quarter 2026 REGENXBIO Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please be advised that today's conference is being recorded. I will now turn the conference over to Patrick Christmas, Chief Legal Officer of REGENXBIO. Please go ahead.

Patrick Christmas: Good morning, and thank you for joining us today. Earlier this morning, REGENXBIO released financial and operating results for the third quarter ended September 30, 2026. The press release is available on our website at www.regenxbio.com. Today's conference call will include forward-looking statements regarding our financial outlook in addition to regulatory and product development plans. These forward-looking statements are subject to risks and uncertainties that may cause actual results to differ from those forecasted and can be identified by words such as expect, plan, will, may, anticipate, believe, should, intend and other words of similar meaning. Any such forward-looking statements are not guarantees of future performance and involve certain risks and uncertainties.

These risks are described in the Risk Factors and the Management's Discussion and Analysis section of REGENXBIO's annual report on Form 10-K for the full year ended December 31, 2025, and comparable Risk Factors sections of REGENXBIO's quarterly report on Form 10-Q, which will be on file with the Securities and Exchange Commission and available on the SEC's website. Any information we provide on this conference call is provided only as of the date of this call, November 5, 2026, and we undertake no obligation to update any forward-looking statements we may make on this call on account of new information, future events or otherwise. Please be advised that today's call is being recorded and webcast.

In addition, any unaudited or pro forma financial information that may be provided is preliminary and does not purport to project financial positions or operating results of the company. Actual results may differ materially. I'll now turn the call to Curran Simpson, President and CEO of REGENXBIO. Curran?

Curran Simpson: Thank you, Patrick, and good morning, everyone. Thank you for joining us today. This is an exciting time for REGENXBIO. We are on the cusp of multiple potential regulatory approvals and are preparing for commercialization. Joining me today are Dr. Steve Pakola, our Chief Medical Officer; and Mitch Chan, our Chief Financial Officer. I'll provide an update on our lead programs before turning it over to them for additional details. I'll begin with sura-vec, our potential first-in-class gene therapy for wet AMD and diabetic retinopathy developed in collaboration with AbbVie. Ahead of our top line pivotal readout expected later this year, last month, we hosted an investor event featuring 5 leading retina specialists.

They discussed the opportunity for sura-vec to transform the wet AMD treatment paradigm. I want to highlight 3 key takeaways from the event. First, the unmet need in wet AMD remains significant. While anti-VEGF therapies are highly effective, the burden of frequent injections can be difficult for patients to sustain over time. As a result, patients who are unable to maintain consistent treatment schedules may not realize the full potential benefit of today's standard of care and subsequently lose vision. Second, sura-vec has the potential to benefit a broad spectrum of patients, including those with high disease activity and those with bilateral disease.

During our event, the physicians reinforced the advantages of a onetime treatment across the range of wet AMD patients they treat and pointed to patients with high disease burden as ideal candidates for gene therapy. We and AbbVie incorporated these patients in our clinical studies, giving us a potential advantage in obtaining a broad label and treating these patients commercially. Sura-vec pivotal studies include a broad population of patients, including newly diagnosed and treatment-experienced patients. Additionally, sura-vec is the only late-stage gene therapy program with reported clinical data supporting treatment in both eyes. Third, sura-vec is well positioned for commercial adoption. The commercial infrastructure for subretinal delivery already exists due to LUXTURNA and other approved therapies.

Further, we know the majority of retina specialists performing these procedures are at high-volume centers, many of whom are included in our pivotal studies. We and AbbVie have already laid a significant foundation having trained over 500 retina specialists to date. Together, these factors, combined with AbbVie's global reach and leading eye care capabilities, give us confidence as we proceed towards potential commercial launch. We expect top line results from both ATMOSPHERE and ASCENT this quarter, followed by global regulatory submissions in 2027. Looking ahead, we see wet AMD as the tip of the spear in terms of transforming the treatment paradigm for chronic retinal diseases. We are currently enrolling in the Phase IIb/III NAAVIGATE study in diabetic retinopathy.

Current anti-VEGF therapy is vastly underused in DR due to the treatment burden for patients who are predominantly middle-aged working adults. We believe a onetime gene therapy has the potential to address this large unmet need and represents another potential blockbuster opportunity for sura-vec. Turning to RGX-202. We continue to make meaningful progress towards bringing our potential best-in-class gene therapy for Duchenne to patients in the U.S. and beyond. Preparations are well underway to submit the BLA under the accelerated approval pathway in the first quarter of 2027, supporting potential approval in the U.S. in the second half of 2027. As a reminder, the BLA is expected to include data from both our pivotal and confirmatory studies.

Outside of the U.S., momentum for RGX-202 continues, and we remain on track to initiate our ex-U.S. randomized placebo-controlled study named AFFINITY RISE in the first half of 2027. We continue to be highly encouraged by the impressive microdystrophin, functional and safety data to date, which demonstrate the potential of RGX-202 to meaningfully improve outcomes for patients with Duchenne. We have strong support from treating physicians, families and advocacy organizations. At our FDA-inspected in-house manufacturing innovation center, we continue to manufacture intended commercial supply in anticipation of a potential 2027 commercial launch. We are sharply focused on advancing our late-stage pipeline, executing against upcoming milestones and making disciplined investment decisions that support these priorities.

Steve Pakola: Thank you, Curran, and good morning, everyone. I'll begin by speaking to our retina franchise, sura-vec, which is being developed in both wet AMD and diabetic retinopathy with our partner, AbbVie. As we approach top line data later this quarter, let me review the design of our 2 pivotal trials, ATMOSPHERE and ASCENT. Together, these trials have enrolled over 1,300 patients across 14 countries and represent the largest gene therapy trials ever conducted. Both trials randomized patients 1:1:1 to low-dose sura-vec, high-dose sura-vec or an on-label anti-VEGF control. The control in ATMOSPHERE is monthly ranibizumab. In ASCENT, the control is aflibercept every 8 weeks.

The primary endpoint in both studies is non-inferiority on change in best corrected visual acuity or BCVA at 54 weeks. Both studies are powered at 90% with more than 600 patients in each. In the Phase I/II study at doses similar to those in the pivotal studies, sura-vec-treated patients demonstrated stable to improved visual acuity and had meaningful reductions in anti-VEGF treatment burden through 5 years. Additionally, sura-vec is the only gene therapy in development to demonstrate the potential to treat both eyes. We believe the ability to target the immune-privileged subretinal space is a significant advantage for patients and physicians and could meaningfully differentiate sura-vec from other investigational gene therapies.

We look forward to reporting top line results by year-end and expect this joint announcement with AbbVie to highlight injection burden and anatomy in addition to the primary endpoint. Turning to diabetic retinopathy, where we continue to advance our pivotal development program. Earlier this year, we dosed the first patient in the Phase IIb/III NAAVIGATE study, which triggered a $100 million milestone payment from AbbVie. More recently, at the Retina Society Annual Meeting, we presented encouraging 3-year results from the Phase II ALTITUDE trial. The new data highlighted long-term results from dose level 3, the same dose being evaluated in NAAVIGATE.

At 3 years, 60% of patients improved by at least 2 steps on the diabetic retinopathy severity scale without additional treatment. And 3 of 4 patients who improved 1 step at 1 year without additional treatment went on to improve by at least 2 steps by year 3. These findings reinforce the potential of sura-vec to change the treatment landscape for the millions of patients with non-proliferative diabetic retinopathy. Turning to RGX-121. We continue to collect and evaluate additional patient imaging and long-term follow-up data with the goal of getting this important treatment to patients.

We are working closely with the FDA and with the support of our partner, NS Pharma, and we'll update you on the path forward as we progress. Within the rare disease franchise, our primary focus remains on our potential best-in-class therapy for Duchenne, RGX-202. RGX-202 was designed to maximize potential benefit through the combination of 3 key elements: one, our novel microdystrophin. The inclusion of the CT domain makes RGX-202 the closest construct to naturally occurring dystrophin; two, a proactive immune suppression regimen; and three, a commercial-ready manufacturing process that delivers industry-leading product purity. As reported in May, our top line pivotal results demonstrated robust microdystrophin expression, encouraging functional improvement and a favorable safety profile.

These positive results, together with the statistically significant strong correlation observed between microdystrophin expression and NSAA improvement at 1 year will be key components of our upcoming BLA submission. Our BLA is expected to include safety data from more than 60 patients across the pivotal and confirmatory studies. It's important to remember that our analysis plans for the pivotal study were prespecified and have not changed. The confirmatory study has already completed dosing and the vast majority of patients in the pivotal and confirmatory studies received RGX-202 using the intended commercial manufacturing process. These are all factors that we believe contribute to the strength of our planned BLA.

Outside the U.S., we continue to make progress towards initiating the AFFINITY RISE global double-masked, placebo-controlled randomized trial that is designed to enroll approximately 100 patients with a 2:1 active to placebo randomization. We expect this study to initiate in the first half of next year. I want to thank our investigators and the patients and families who make our progress possible. With that, I'll turn the call over to Mitch to review our financial results.

Mitchell Chan: Thank you, Steve, and good morning, everyone. REGENXBIO ended the third quarter of 2026 with cash, cash equivalents and marketable securities of $251 million compared to $241 million as of December 31, 2025. The increase was due to a $100 million milestone payment received from AbbVie in July 2026 for the dosing of the first patient in the Phase IIb/III NAAVIGATE study and the net proceeds of $108 million from an underwritten public offering of common stocks and prefunded warrants completed in July 2026. This total excludes a $39 million payment received in October 2026 pursuant to a settlement agreement with Sarepta Therapeutics.

Our cash runway is late into fourth quarter of 2027 beyond multiple milestones, including top line data in wet AMD, the BLA submissions for RGX-202 and sura-vec in wet AMD and the expected PDUFA date for RGX-202. This cash runway guidance does not include the receipt of any potential proceeds associated with other potential non-dilutive sources of funding. We continue to receive ZOLGENSMA royalties in select OUS territories and ITVISMA royalties in the U.S. and certain OUS territories. We find ourselves well positioned as we advance towards multiple potential product launches. With that, I will turn the call back to Curran to provide final thoughts.

Curran Simpson: Thank you, Mitch. Before we turn to Q&A, I want to reiterate our excitement as we approach key milestones in our lead programs through the remainder of 2026. The sura-vec pivotal top line data expected this quarter could reshape the treatment paradigm for wet AMD. Steve Pakola: Sure. Annabel, thanks for the questions. Yes, the fellow eye aspect actually has always been a critical component for our program, and it's an example of how comprehensive our global program has been. And I think a key aspect is we're really differentiated here in the ability to treat the fellow eye. So this is one of the benefits of the immune privileged subretinal delivery that you can have more comfort from a safety and efficacy standpoint to treat both eyes. And that's very relevant, not just for the FDA, but of course, all of the treatment community, the retina specialists because roughly half of patients, not surprisingly, do have or will develop fellow eye disease.

So a treating clinician wants to know they can not only treat the first eye, but also have the opportunity to have onetime treatment for their patients in the fellow eye. Curran Simpson: Graig, happy to comment on that. I think it starts out with the initial design of the construct, which includes the C-terminus. So from the very beginning, we wanted to bring a differentiated product into clinical development. And the C-terminus in our preclinical data showed a clear benefit from a microdystrophin construct that's devoid of the C-terminus. And we think that's what's carrying over into the clinical study. The other elements of differentiation, I think, that are critical is, I'd say, second, the manufacturing process, which delivers a capsid at 80% purity also at a great cost of goods. It's hard to get both, but we've been able to achieve that.

We've invested in CMC for over 7 years, and now we're bearing the fruits of that for this program. So I think that's the second element, which we believe not only helps with the high microdystrophin levels we're seeing in patients' biopsies, but also on safety. And then that leads to the last leg of the stool is the immune suppression regimen that's been developed for this program, as I mentioned earlier, has allowed us to drastically reduce the liver toxicity events seen with patients.

But more importantly, and I think the long-term value of the program is it allows us to deliver the maximum dose of product to give the Duchenne boys the maximum potential benefit of long-term functional benefit. And I think that was the aim is to match our preclinical data where we saw tremendous restoration of function in the mdx mouse models. We were able to get to that dose because of that IS regimen. And now we're seeing it in our functional data, which does differentiate it from our competitor.

We still, to this day, haven't seen anything to compare to from our competitor, whereas I think we've been very transparent and proud of our functional data and the fact that it correlates to the microdystrophin levels.

Maybe this is more of a Sarepta question, but how prevalent is it becoming among physicians systemic gene therapy to just immune sirolimus and eculizumab given some of the safety events here?

Curran Simpson: Thanks. Yes, I think that safety, in particular for an accelerated approval approach is absolutely critical. And I think what we've shown in our pivotal data set on safety is highly encouraging. Again, we don't really see liver enzyme elevation through the course of treatment. And again, keeping in mind that we're treating at 2E14 per kilo which is the maximum dose we wanted to attain based on our preclinical data. So that, in return, gives patients the best possible chance, I believe, for functional benefit. I think FDA will have this as top of mind. I think our data set will be in line with that.

We do know that other companies are adjusting their IS regimen in the field, in clinical studies, et cetera. We haven't had to do that in any of our studies. We've been consistent throughout our pivotal and confirmatory study patients using the same exact immunosuppression regimen. So I feel like that's a strength for our program. It's the consistency of product, consistency of safety outcomes and the fact that the patients are all doing well. I think it's important to maybe look 2 years out, though and say, what if all of these companies, including ourselves, equalize on safety because we learn over time how to deliver AAV more safely.

That's where I think we're still going to win because we have the C-terminus. We have the highest purity, I believe, in the field. And what the market will react to then is what will deliver the best efficacy if safety is sort of the same across programs. And I believe we're in a position to win based on the data we published and the reaction we're getting, not so much in just the functional assessments, but what we hear from investigators. I can let Steve maybe comment a little bit on what we're hearing in the field as well on this topic.

Steve Pakola: Yes. I think one of the nice things is, of course, we have actual experience doing this. And as Curran has highlighted, right from the beginning, we've used this regimen. And I think the aspect that comes here is if you look at the overall benefit risk equation, certainly, patients and patient families want safety and they also want to have their best shot on goal because they literally only have one shot. As soon as you take a gene therapy, there's no chance to get a second one. So you really want to have an optimal chance of success, and that's where all the differentiators that Curran mentioned come in.

And I think on your question of the regulatory view on this, it really is the highest sensitivity in the field of the liver. And I think the differentiation there is so clear it really adds up to a really strong case for us as far as benefit over and above existing therapy.

Operator: The next question comes from the line of Luca Issi with RBC.

Unknown Analyst: Congrats on all the progress. This is [ Cassie ] for Luca. Curran, as you have elaborated on and highlighted already, the pivotal data showed a highly statistically significant correlation between week 12 microdystrophin expression and NSAA improvement at 1 year in 9 patients. So now as you move towards your pre-BLA meeting, how do you expect the FDA, especially now under new leadership to weigh this correlation given you were the first to show this data and evidence is only now in single-digit number of patients. Would there be a threshold or a similar degree of correlation you expect they expect? And lastly, the correlation was demonstrated on NSAA as a functional measure.

Can you speak to the time function test if those are also showing a similar picture and if such data will be included in the BLA package?

Curran Simpson: Great question. We do think that the review team will be excited about the correlation between the 2 because I think it ultimately speaks to the drug effect. And I think in a single-arm study, the things that we hear, in particular, I think Karim Mikhail talking about at FDA, where they're looking at updating regulatory frameworks for rare disease. What we're bringing to the table in terms of our data set is directly in line with what we're hearing from FDA leadership. And that would be a few things. One is magnitude of effect.

I believe our NSAA data to date shows a dramatic magnitude of effect advantage, particularly in the older patients where they would normally be in a decline phase. And then I think, again, the second is in a single-arm study, the ideal outcome is that you can correlate your biomarker to a clinical benefit that underscores that your biomarker is reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit. And again, I think that's directly in line with what we're hearing from FDA.

So I think as FDA focuses on rare disease, which I believe they are, and as we bring forward the data that we've shown and the additional data that will be part of the BLA submission, those 2 are directly in line with each other. Let me let Steve talk just a little bit about what we're seeing in other -- some of the other time function tests.

Steve Pakola: Yes. So one of the strengths of the functional data that we've released at the top line standpoint is not only the magnitude of effect, but the consistency of effect. where we've seen functional benefit in not just the broad NSAA that traditionally people have thought is not so sensitive, but yet we've seen that effect. We've seen that effect across different ways of different methods of controlling. And then we've also seen consistent effect across the time function test with a particular focus on time to stand as the traditional one that is of most interest. We'll have more patients, 14 patients for the BLA. So we'll look for continued nice magnitude of effect and totality of evidence.

And since we've seen this strong correlation with NSAA, the broad measure, yes, we'll also be assessing correlation with the time function test. We feel good about this overall analysis because of how consistent the magnitude of effect was.

Curran Simpson: I think one thing just to add to Steve's note is in some of the communications FDA has sent our way, they have acknowledged the difference in microdystrophin that our product is unique and contains the C-terminus. So they're looking at it, I believe, as a unique construct that deserves a unique review.

Operator: Our next question comes from the line of Sean McCutcheon with Raymond James.

Sean McCutcheon: Can you speak to the randomization criteria, patient disposition and rescue criteria for ATMOSPHERE and ASCENT? How this differs to competitor pivotal studies for extended treatment interval therapies? And how that informs expectations for BCVA stability and interpretation of treatment burden reduction?

Curran Simpson: Sean, happy to do that. I'll let Steve give the specifics.

Steve Pakola: Sure. Sean, so you hit on a lot of the key aspects that certainly were part of our prospective planning. And I think the key aspect is we wanted to have clinically relevant data. So for us, as far as the patients we enrolled and randomized our goal was to address those patients that the clinicians told us they were eager to be able to treat with gene therapy, a onetime opportunity. And we hear over and over again that the big unmet need is the patients who have high disease activity, and therefore, have still frequent injections even with the newer agents, and that's roughly in the 40% population.

So if you want to address that unmet need, you have to actually evaluate it in your Phase II and particularly your pivotal study. So we include treatment-experienced patients, including those patients with high disease activity. And we also use screening criteria where, yes, you have to show that there's a response to ranibizumab injection. But we also look 1 week after dosing where that's going to allow you to enroll not just patients who are responsive, but also these patients who, yes, they respond at 1 week, but they may have disease recurrence very quickly. And therefore, those are exactly the kind of patients you'd want to enroll.

So the other benefit is we use the same inclusion, exclusion as our bioreactor pharmacodynamic study and also the same rescue criteria. And that gives us confidence, because in that study, we saw stable to improve vision, stable disease control and a very high reduction in treatment burden, including patients not needing any injections at a year. You mentioned the rescue criteria. So here, again, same as the bioreactor study. It's all prespecified objective based on change in best corrected visual acuity and/or reduction or worsening of fluid or disease activity measured by OCT. So all these factors give us confidence here. And we powered the studies based on consideration of this patient population.

So the goal, again, was having positive studies, but also clear evidence in the patients that we care most about. So it really gives an example of how comprehensive our programs are in wet AMD, where they're not just large, they're not just global, but they're also looking at the patients that the clinicians care about.

Operator: Our next call comes from the line of Ellie Merle with Barclays.

Eliana Merle: Just ahead of the Phase III wet AMD data, I guess, expected imminently, a common investor question is around sort of the effect size on treatment, burden reduction and the proportion of patients that will be injection-free. And how to think about that in the context of the broader gene therapy landscape, given we'll be getting other Phase III trials from multiple other companies next year as well in the gene therapy space. I guess, given that you have a more severe patient population enrolled, I guess, how are you thinking about what would be a competitive proportion of patients that become treatment-free and then the proportion of like percent treatment reduction?

And how to think about how this could compare to some of the other trials that might be enrolling more treatment-naive patients?

Curran Simpson: Thanks, Ellie. I'll start with just -- I think we're in really good shape in terms of alignment with AbbVie that at the outset of the program and really based off our Phase I/II data, we were able to develop a target product profile that is in full alignment between us on those analyses. So I'll let Steve comment a little bit on what expectations we have in terms of sort of what good looks like in the studies.

Steve Pakola: Yes. Ellie, that's a great question because context matters here. You can't really go across studies and compare nominally treatment reductions without taking into account how tough-to-treat the patients are that you're treating in your trial. And as we discussed earlier, we have the opportunity based on earlier results to go after the clinically relevant population. So -- the goal is really to have at least a 50% reduction in treatment burden with a meaningful proportion of those patients not needing any injection. And the key is that if we hit that mark, we're hitting it in a very tough-to-treat population.

So that's the evidence that the clinicians need and the patients need in order to make their decision on treatment.

Curran Simpson: I think one other aspect that is important to that, that we've gotten information from following a number of retinal specialists is that beyond -- if the program shows beyond 12-month durability, there's a high level of interest in gene therapy. And just as a reminder, we're out 5 years on some patients. So I think the element of durability that's associated with sort of value and trust in our program, we're going to have excellent data at the time of review and eventual disclosure to support all of that.

Operator: Our next question comes from the line of Yi Chen with H.C. Wainwright.

Yi Chen: Could you please clarify what decisions the FDA has to make post the pre-BLA meeting to enable the accelerated approval of RGX-202?

Curran Simpson: Yi, thanks for the question. The main decision and one of the main questions that the topic of the pre-BLA meeting is does the data set that we have in hand qualify for review under the accelerated approval pathway. And obviously, we'll have, I think, a reasonable understanding of their position on that coming out of the pre-BLA meeting. But the true stamp on that is 2 months post filing that they accept the BLA for review. And then it really becomes a review process through the next 6 months to go in depth through the data and support that review process.

But I think the key decision point coming up, and we'll get some color on that through the pre-BLA process is acceptance of the BLA under the AA pathway. And we expect that about 2 months after submission.

Operator: I am showing no further questions. And at this time, this does conclude the question-and-answer session. Thank you for your participation in today's conference. This does conclude the program. You may now disconnect.

Meta’s warns that election backlash against data centers could make AI expansion harder

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Meta President Dina Powell McCormick said that the Democratic wins in the midterm elections are going to make it a lot harder for companies to build data centers, adding that the industry has “not done as good a job as we needed to explain the benefits of these investments.”

Speaking at the WSJ Tech conference on Wednesday, the Meta exec pointed out that the backlash against data centers — which was very much on the ballot during this election season — was also tied to people’s feelings about AI technology overall.

“The visible sort of manifestation of AI is a data center, and it’s something that people can say, ‘I don’t want this in my community,’” she explained. “I think there’s an underlying bigger issue, which is how people feel about AI in general.”

Meta believes it could turn around the negative consumer sentiment around AI if it’s able to demonstrate the value, not only of the data centers coming to local communities, but in the technology itself.

“The stakes of who builds the technology that cures cancer, that gives access to education, that is also a very fundamental difference…Mark [Zuckerberg, Meta CEO] is very ‘long human,’ and he’s betting on humans more than machines,” Powell McCormick explained. “And that’s what we mean when we say the future is for everyone. That people should not be left behind. But the way to ensure that is that this technology is democratized, that people have access to it in a very fair and transparent way,” she said.

The exec also touted Meta’s experience in building data centers, saying that the company has been doing so for 20 years, as it takes a lot of compute power to offer the products it does in the social networking space. Today, the company has data centers in 23 states, she said, and five of them, including one in Texas, are planned as projects of one gigawatt or more.

But Meta is now worried that it could lose some of its investments after politicians ran on anti-data-center platforms during the midterms.

“I think it’s going to be harder,” Powell McCormick said, describing what the future of building data centers looks like. “It has been hard, I should say, but I also think that it’s becoming clear what models work, and the states want those models that work.”

Specifically, communities only want data centers when it benefits them in some way, she explained, laying out the approach that Meta today uses to close its data center deals in local communities.

Successful projects see Meta paying for its own electricity, directing any tax surpluses as a result of the data centers toward teachers and first responders, keeping the labor force local, and getting bipartisan support for the project, among other things.

Going forward, she expects data center projects to come under more review and be weighed against whether the constituents actually want them.

Meta has pinned its hopes on its AI investments, particularly its consumer AI agent Muse scaling far beyond the 10 million users it has today, to become a business that one day serves billions. But even if that’s not the case, it believes that its investments in data centers will not be for nothing, as the compute itself will be valuable.

“We’re getting calls every day to lease that compute [we’ve built], and so one way or another, we’re seeing a path to monetization,” Powell McCormick said.

Bluetooth Speeds Are Set to Quadruple in 2027

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The Bluetooth Group has announced a new standard. Here's how it works and when you can expect to benefit from it.

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Wireless tech is about to get a whole lot snappier. On Thursday, the Bluetooth Special Interest Group, the trade association that oversees Bluetooth, officially unveiled Bluetooth High Data Throughput, a flagship upgrade set to arrive as part of the Bluetooth Core 7.0 standard. The revision supercharges Bluetooth Low Energy by pushing maximum bit rates to 7.5 Mbps, effectively quadrupling the throughput of the existing LE 2M technology. Compatible devices are slated to hit store shelves starting in 2027.

That’s good news for phone users, tablet havers, laptop groupies, smart speaker stans and more. Bluetooth is still the most popular medium-to-short-range wireless standard for connecting devices. But this is a big rollout, and the changes will start with premium wireless, such as earbuds, with other applications following.

Those other applications could include spatial computing, exchanging AI-related info with local devices, connecting to health wearables and a host of smart home applications, like playing Spotify through an Amazon Echo Studio or dimming a smart bulb.

“Imagine a smartwatch that syncs hours’ worth of sensor-rich training data to your phone in seconds, smart glasses that deliver richer AI experiences by offloading data for processing, or a smart home product that completes a firmware update with virtually no user-visible downtime,” Øyvind Strøm, executive vice president of the short-range business unit at Nordic Semiconductor, said in a statement. “Bluetooth HDT paves the way for more sophisticated, data-intensive Bluetooth applications.”

A representative from the Bluetooth Special Interest Group didn’t respond to a request for comment.

The biggest change in this update is a new physical layer for Bluetooth. Layers are like different sections of the Bluetooth protocol, and the physical layer is the bedrock of the standard, the foundational layer involved in transmitting and receiving data signals.

Improvements in the physical layer can have very significant effects on all Bluetooth communications. In this case, new modulation schemes let the physical layer handle data more efficiently and transmit more data at once, which is why the speed increases are so significant.

These improvements also make the new standard more efficient, saving battery life in many applications. So your smart lock may last longer before you need to exchange the AA batteries, earbuds won’t need to go back in the case as fast, and so on.

We know the first flagship devices are expected to appear with the technology in early 2027, but don’t have an exact date.

All we know about which specific devices will get the technology first is that it will start with wireless audio.

With protocol changes like this, it can take some time for new products to reach the market, and manufacturers may take a long time, even years, to adopt it.

Someone Finally Asked Matt Damon When He Knew The Odyssey Would Be A Hit. His Answer Was Really Satisfying

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Digger Took A Big, Bold Swing. John Goodman Thinks It Was ‘Killed’ Before It Even Opened

I Just Found Out What Christopher Nolan Made Universal’s Head Honcho Do When He Pitched The Odyssey

Digger Has A Major Plot Twist That's Divided Audiences. I Had To Know How The Cast Feels

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Based on Christopher Nolan’s filmography alone, I always assumed The Odyssey would be a hit. Then, when his stacked cast got announced, there was no doubt in my mind this movie would be a very big deal. Now that it is out and a certified megahit, I’ve been wondering if Matt Damon also knew this book-to-screen adaptation of Homer’s epic would be a phenomenon. Well, now he’s answered that question, and he did it in a way that’s really satisfying.

It always seemed like The Odyssey was “destined to be a smash hit,” as a reporter from GQ put it. However, when he said those words to Damon, the actor responded by saying, “There are no sure things, man.” That led to the question of when exactly the Ocean’s Eleven actor knew he’d been part of a major sensation. Here’s what the Oscar winner had to say:

I felt it when I saw it. There is that thing that can happen where you make a movie, and you feel like it’s really good, and it just doesn’t connect, and there’s just nothing you can do about that. But as the audience reactions started to come in and the critical reactions were great as well, it was like, Okay….

This certainly makes sense. I’m sure everyone can relate to the feeling of thinking you’ve done something great and then finding out it didn’t get the reactions you expected. However, in the case of The Odyssey, it was outstanding, and Damon realized that when he saw it. That fact was then solidified when The Odyssey’s first reactions came out, and then it was really validated when its stellar reviews were published.

Then, the box office made it abundantly clear that this film was one of the biggest hits on the 2026 movie schedule. Overall, The Odyssey made $1.7 billion worldwide, and $625 million domestically. At the time of this writing, that makes it the second-highest-grossing movie of the year behind Spider-Man: Brand New Day.

However, again, while this seemed like it would happen, anything could have happened. So, it makes sense why Damon wasn’t super quick to say he’d made a hit. It also makes sense why Christopher Nolan got nervous right before the movie premiered, as the Odysseus actor explained:

Chris [Nolan], the most nervous he is in the entire process, from the very beginning to the very end, is the eve of the film coming out. He’s genuinely nervous. He talks openly about it, because that is the moment that you put it out into the world and you have to live with the reaction. Ultimately, the audience decides. Once it opened and the responses were what they were, I think I felt like, We’re going to be okay. It was a big bet in the sense that it was a big budget. I’m not used to playing in that arena. The numbers that movies have to get to just to break even at that level are just so beyond the movies that I’m known for.

The Odyssey had a reported budget of $250 million, per Forbes. It also reportedly had a $125 million marketing budget. To break even, the movie had to make between $625 and $750 million, per the outlet. That’s no small feat, and the film managed not only to make its money back, but it ultimately made well over a billion dollars worldwide.

Knowing the risk and the literal cost of this film, it’s satisfying to see Damon talk about how he felt in the lead-up to the release. While it seems he knew what he was making was special, it’s impossible to know that’s the case until it’s done and the world has seen it. And for him to recall what it was like to see audiences embrace this movie is very cool.

Well, now the world has seen it, and people love it. Thankfully, the actor and director know that, too. Now, I have a feeling this phenomenon will continue into awards season, as The Odyssey and Damon are likely going to be major players in next year’s Oscars race. Which leads me to the question: When will Matt Damon realize he could be a contender for an Academy Award?

7 Netflix Mystery Shows That Hook You in the First Scene

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Leading the streaming crusade, Netflix has stood at the top of excellent content. From legendary shows during its early days like Orange Is the New Black to modern masterpieces like The Diplomat, viewers seek brilliant, risky storytelling for their binge-watching pleasure. The thrill of a good series is being drawn in so much that you'll keep watching until the very end. When it comes to Netflix's mystery options, a handful hook you within the first scene.

We are here to honor the incredible mystery-centric shows that hook you instantly. From a terrifying, disorienting wake-up that establishes the entire story to the discovery of a body that sets the mystery into hyperdrive, these shows master the art of immediate intrigue. While Netflix has some perfect shows that are incredible all the way through, they don't have the same brilliant hook as these seven entries.

Sometimes high-concept narratives scare people away, but Bodies is a highly addictive puzzle that pulls you in instantly. Created by Paul Tomalin, Bodies is based on the DC Vertigo graphic novel of the same name. The eight-episode sci-fi mystery thriller follows four different detectives in four distinct eras who all discover the exact same unidentified body in a London alleyway. As their investigations unfold across time, they realize their cases are deeply interconnected. Together, they must unravel a massive, century-spanning conspiracy that threatens humanity's future. A truly brilliant and gripping thriller, Bodies succeeds because you're dropped right into the seismic action.

To kick off the story, streetlights in London begin to flicker and pop just before Detective Sergeant Shahara Hasan (Amaka Okafor) is deployed to police a far-right protest. While monitoring the tense crowd, Shahara spots a suspicious teenage boy hiding behind a bin and carrying a gun. Against orders, she chases him through the streets until he leads her into Longharvest Lane in Whitechapel. There, instead of a simple cornered suspect, she discovers a naked, dead white male body lying on the ground with a gunshot wound to the eye. The victim doesn't just lie there cold—he gasps out a terrified, reality-bending statement before bolting, instantly elevating the mystery from a standard murder to something deeply unsettling. By plunging the audience into high-stakes, chaotic action that defies conventional crime-drama logic, the premiere makes it clear this mystery is unlike any other thriller you've seen. Rather than lingering as a slow-burn procedural, the premiere swiftly layers in parallel historical and futuristic teasers, creating an immediate puzzle of time and causality. It's an adrenaline-fueled opening that packs a thrilling punch.

While many people point to Squid Game as the foreign language thriller that changed the game, the German sci-fi mystery thriller Dark paved the way first. Created by Baran bo Odar and Jantje Friese, Dark follows four interconnected families in the small town of Winden as a child's disappearance uncovers a multi-generational time-travel conspiracy. As the characters search for the truth across linked timelines in 1953, 1986, and 2019, they become trapped in a deterministic loop of fate and cause and effect. For three strong seasons, Dark served as a brilliant time-travel drama that explores the existential implications of time and its effect on human nature and life.

In the first scene of the series, a voice-over recites a quote by Albert Einstein about time being an illusion, accompanied by a montage of ticking clocks, a complex cork board of interconnected strings and photos, and a dark study room. The sequence then moves directly to November 19, 2019, where Michael Kahnwald (Sebastian Rudolph) stands in his attic, seals a mysterious letter with strict instructions—"Do not open before November 4th at 10:13 p.m."—looks at a family photograph, and commits suicide by hanging himself. The shocking scene subverts the standard true-crime missing-persons formula and replaces it with cold, existential dread. The scene follows a calculated, deeply unsettling thematic blueprint that lets you know you are watching a tragedy already in motion. The camera lingers on this devastating, quiet moment, leaving you with urgent questions: why he did it, what's in the letter, and the significance of that specific date. By the time the hypnotic, mirrored opening credits roll to the tune of Apparat's "Goodbye," the show had already laid out its entire central puzzle.

A truly unique entry into the true crime genre, the psychological crime thriller Mindhunter, created by Joe Penhall, is based on Mindhunter: Inside the FBI's Elite Serial Crime Unit by John E. Douglas and Mark Olshaker. The series follows FBI agents Holden Ford (Jonathan Groff) and Bill Tench (Holt McCallany) as they pioneer the Behavioral Science Unit. Alongside psychologist Dr. Wendy Carr (Anna Torv), they interview incarcerated serial killers to understand their psychology and apply these insights to active murder cases. The series also explores the heavy emotional toll and institutional skepticism the team faces as it builds the foundation of modern criminal profiling. Tackling the crime genre in a unique way, this two-season series introduces a new way to analyze crime stories.

The series opens in Braddock, Pennsylvania, in 1977 with a tense hostage standoff that serves as the catalyst for the entire series. A deeply disturbed man named Cody Miller (David H. Holmes) robs a liquor store, steals a shotgun, and barricades himself inside a building with a woman and several other hostages. As local police bark orders, young FBI Special Agent Holden Ford arrives as the hostage negotiator. Holden tries to de-escalate the situation by establishing an empathetic, human connection with the suspect. He taps into the suspect's delusion as Cody demands to see his wife and begins shouting that he is literally invisible. Holden speaks softly, telling Cody that he can see he is cold, which coaxes Cody to pull his pants back up and take his primary hostage back inside. Holden learns Cody's wife is arriving, but advises against letting them speak. Realizing his demands aren't being met and that his "invisibility" is failing, Cody suddenly stops listening to Holden and blows his own head off with a shotgun. The scene closes with Holden returning home covered in blood, trying and failing to scrub the stain out of his white dress shirt.

The scene establishes the series' central conflict as Holden doesn't see the result as a success, left obsessed with the realization that traditional, authoritative FBI methods fail against abnormal psychology. Directed by David Fincher, the sudden, graphic shock instantly breaks traditional television formulas and signals to the audience that no one is safe. Holden's failure will eventually become his success, as the traditional rules of law enforcement are entirely useless against a changing landscape of irrational, deeply broken minds.

A seemingly forgotten series, The Madness is an action conspiracy thriller unlike anything you've ever experienced. Created by Stephen Belber, The Madness follows Muncie Daniels (Colman Domingo), a prominent media pundit and political commentator. While on a solo writing retreat in the Pocono Mountains, Muncie stumbles upon the body of a murdered white supremacist and is framed for the assassination. Forced to go on the run as a fugitive, he must navigate a web of mass disinformation and expose a deep political conspiracy to clear his name and keep his family safe. A high-octane thrill ride, The Madness is all about Domingo and his dominating performance from the jump.

In the opening scene, Muncie wakes up disoriented and alone in the Poconos woods, stumbles onto a nearby roadway, and flags down a ride. He goes to a local family restaurant where patrons stare at him, and he tells police officers Wagner (Hamish Allan-Headley) and Lutz (Dylan Taylor) he's had a rough morning. Suddenly, flashes of memory surface before he transitions to getting makeup done and preparing to host his CNN-style television program. The scene immediately grips you by using a flash-forward technique that forces instant empathy and high-stakes mystery. Rather than build to the crisis, the series drops you directly into the terrifying aftermath of a violent event. Injured, bloodied, and completely disoriented, the audience is instantly placed in his exact shoes as the protagonist. The peace is shattered as Muncie is shown frantically sprinting through the woods while a gunshot rings out in the distance. The shaky, claustrophobic cinematography mimics a raw survival instinct, generating immediate adrenaline and a palpable sense of danger. Then, by moving from frantic chaos to Muncie's normal life, you are desperate to figure out how an articulate, high-profile media analyst could possibly end up running for his life in a remote swamp.

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

One of the greatest long-running Netflix series is The Night Agent. The highly addictive series, created by Shawn Ryan and based on the novel of the same name by Matthew Quirk, follows Peter Sutherland (Gabriel Basso), a low-level FBI agent who works in the basement of the White House monitoring a phone line that never rings—until the night it does. When a panicked tech CEO calls, Peter is thrust into a sprawling, fast-paced conspiracy involving a Russian mole at the highest levels of the U.S. government. As he runs to protect the witness and clear his own name, he uncovers secrets that lead all the way to the Oval Office. Moving into its fourth and final season, The Night Agent became an instant hit thanks to its simple yet evocative start.

In the opening sequence, FBI agent Peter Sutherland boards a DC Metro train during his commute and notices a suspicious man leaving a backpack behind, which he discovers contains a timer-triggered bomb. He immediately forces the train to make an emergency stop in a dark tunnel and helps panic-stricken passengers escape into the underground structures. The bomb detonates moments later, sending a massive fireball through the tunnel and killing one civilian while saving dozens of others thanks to Peter's quick actions. While recovering from the blast, Peter spots the bomber standing among the bystanders on the surface and gives chase, though the suspect manages to elude him after Peter is hurt in a car accident. This high-octane start raises the stakes beyond his life-saving moment. It paints the protagonist as a competent, brave, and quick-thinking hero. The frantic life-or-death scale of the flashback contrasts sharply with the quiet, mundane reality of his day job: sitting in a dark White House basement waiting for a silent emergency phone to ring. This quick-fire tragedy gives Peter instant sympathetic context and raises the emotional weight of every political secret and threat that follows.​​​​​​​

Easily the biggest travesty of 2025 was the cancellation of The Residence, as it was simply perfect. But while many fans mourn the loss of a brilliant vehicle for Uzo Aduba, let's honor why it was so good. Created by Paul William Davies and inspired by The Residence: Inside the Private World of the White House by Kate Andersen Brower, the Shondaland series is a comedic murder mystery set in the White House during a high-stakes state dinner. When the White House Chief Usher, A. B. Wynter (Giancarlo Esposito), is found dead, an eccentric and brilliant detective named Cordelia Cupp (Aduba) is brought in to solve the crime, sifting through 157 suspects among the mansion's staff and guests. The show follows Cupp as she teams up with skeptical FBI agent Edwin Park (Randall Park) to untangle the "upstairs, downstairs" whodunit. The Residence is a fun, cozy, campy romp that delivers the right amount of mystery from the start.

In the opening scene, A.B. Wynter is found dead by the president's mother-in-law, Nan Cox (Jane Curtin), at 9:57 p.m. after a strange thump is heard. The death is immediately presented as an apparent suicide, with slit wrists and a suicide note. The president's chief adviser, Harry Hollinger (Ken Marino), and other officials try to rush a suicide ruling to avoid a political scandal during the state visit. But once the eccentric Cordelia Cupp arrives, she quickly spots fatal inconsistencies, including signs of poisoning and evidence that the body was moved. The first scene hooks you because of its abrupt, jarring contrast between extreme high-society elegance and sudden, chaotic horror. However, the show immediately subverts this stately elegance by ripping the audience up to the third floor. The story instantly becomes the ultimate locked-room mystery where everyone, even world leaders, is potential suspects. It thrusts the story into the messy inner workings of the staff who have just lost an integral cog of the building. With the arrival of Cupp, it's clear she will not play by the average rules, immediately leading you to adoring the lovable bird-watching underdog. ​​​​​​​

A lesser appreciated crime drama, Untamed takes its audience to a beautiful local for a dark tale. Created by Mark L. Smith and Elle Smith, the thriller follows Kyle Turner (Eric Bana), a special agent for the National Parks Service, as he investigates the suspicious and brutal death of a woman who fell from El Capitan in Yosemite National Park. Partnered with Naya Vasquez (Lily Santiago), a rookie ranger who recently transferred from Los Angeles, Turner must navigate a lawless wilderness to uncover a deep-seated criminal conspiracy hiding beneath the park's scenic beauty. As the investigation intensifies, the dark secrets of the park force him to confront long-buried trauma from his own troubled past. A wonderful example of suspense and tension building, Untamed hooks you with the shocking event that sets the wheels in motion.

From the beginning, Untamed shows you the majestic locale the story is set in. The camera pans over Yosemite National Park before zooming in on the sheer rock face of El Capitan. Two rock climbers are scaling the cliff when a dead woman's body suddenly plummets from the summit above. The falling body crashes into their gear and gets tangled in their climbing ropes, violently tearing them off their anchors and putting both climbers' lives in immediate danger before they manage to stabilize themselves. It’s an utterly terrifying scene, and while your mind fears for the live ones, you rush to wonder why there is a dead body plummeting. With a breathtaking wilderness scale and immediate, visceral danger, the heart-stopping peril in a beautiful place reminds viewers that danger lurks anywhere. The sequence instantly forces the audience to wonder who the victim is, whether it is a murder or an accident, and how a body ended up at the top of one of the world's most famous rock formations.

OpenAI released a new cybersecurity model. Is that enough?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Max Justicz, the chief technology officer and cofounder of the startup Charge Robotics, used an OpenAI model to find a severe vulnerability that — if exploited — could allow hackers to take control of servers running Ubuntu, a widely-used open-source Linux based operating system.

Justicz, who has a hobbyist’s interest in cybersecurity and previously found a vulnerability in the same codebase, told TechCrunch that he found this issue using GPT 5.6 Sol, released in July, through the AI maker’s Daybreak Blue, a program that gives cybersecurity defenders access to powerful frontier models.

“I literally just aimed ChatGPT at the same body of code and said ‘I found this bug previously that was fixed several years ago, please find bugs as bad or worse,’ and it came back in 2 hours with this,” Justicz said. “It’s extremely convoluted and hard for a human to find, but it works!”

He reported the issue to the Debian Project, which maintains the software in question and issued a patch to fix the vulnerability. There has been no known evidence of exploitation. Still, the ease with which he found it suggests that we’ve only seen the tip of the iceberg when it comes to potential exploits that even novice hackers can utilize with the help of AI models.

“I can only imagine what one could do with more time and access to less neutered models. We should expect some extremely crazy bugs to be found and exploited over the next few months,” Justicz wrote in a social media thread discussing his finding. “This might be obvious to most folks here, but there is currently huge alpha in taking some code or binary someone has not audited yet and just aiming a powerful AI model at it.”

OpenAI appears to have noticed. Today, the AI giant announced a new model focused on cybersecurity, ChatGPT Cyber, would be made available to the highest-tier of users in the cybersecurity program, Daybreak Red, which allows offensive cybersecurity researchers tools for vulnerability hunting and development. Yesterday, OpenAI said that the same model had been used to scan and prepare patches for vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, finding 948 potential issues. OpenAI also found 81 “novel” vulnerabilities in Firefox, the web browser.

“Our goal is to dramatically expand Daybreak, bringing advanced security research to organizations of every size, independent researchers, and open-source maintainers,” the frontier lab said in a statement.

OpenAI has insisted that there is a narrow window of time for the people who safeguard digital infrastructure to take action before AI models become even better at hacking and easier to access. For cyber defenders, however, it can feel like the frontier labs have unleashed a mountain of new work that outstrips their resources. Justicz said that he personally spent about $5,000 on subscriptions and tokens to find several exploits.

Besides Daybreak’s model access, OpenAI has also announced a $1 billion initiative to support cybersecurity professionals in the program who work on critical infrastructure like the electrical grid or water systems.

The Daybreak program has its own critiques. Justicz notes that the frontier lab appears to have recently “nerfed” Daybreak Blue to make it less useful at finding vulnerabilities. One one hand, it may be good to limit access from potential bad actors — Justicz said he just uploaded a photo of his drivers’ license to join the program. On the other, cybersecurity professionals say they have one hand tied if they can’t use frontier technology while bad actors can deploy open-weight models with abliterated guardrails.

Still, OpenAI says just “thousands” of cybersecurity professionals are participating in the Daybreak program, versus a far larger number of potential hackers.

It’s unclear if frontier labs are devoting enough of their resources to this issue. Eric Wallace, a researcher on OpenAI’s technical staff, said that “we are now allocating a significant amount of our frontier compute towards discovering and patching vulnerabilities in open source codebases, fully pro bono.”

But the Linux bug hunt cost $1.2 million, which is significantly less than the approximate $6 million token spend the company invested in finding a potential solution to a famous problem of fluid dynamics.

If there is a narrow window of time to patch the digital infrastructure the world depends on, more urgency may be required.

macOS 27.3 Golden Gate public beta 1 now available, here’s everything new

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Following the release of macOS 27.3 developer beta 1, Apple is now rolling out the first test build for users enrolled in the public beta. Here’s what’s coming

Contrary to macOS 27.2, it looks like Apple had quite a bit in store for macOS 27.3 and its counterparts.

In addition to expanding Siri AI to four more languages, including Traditional Chinese, Norwegian, Turkish, and Vietnamese, the system also introduces several new Safari features, including Vertical Tabs, Adaptive Dark Mode for websites, and the ability to organize and manage tabs using Siri AI.

macOS 27.3 also adds native Markdown rendering to Quick Look, allowing users to preview .md files with proper formatting instead of raw Markdown syntax.

The update also expands Siri AI’s capabilities across Apple’s own apps, allowing users to perform more complex actions in apps like Messages, Music, Calendar, Photos, and Reminders, as well as manage files and folders.

Apple is also introducing new tools for third-party developers to create advanced Siri AI integrations using Markdown-based instructions, allowing the assistant to coordinate multistep actions across different apps.

Users who enjoyed the wallpaper introduced with the new touchscreen MacBook Pro will also find a new screensaver called Pro Lighteensaver, which features light moving along the metallic-looking lines that form the word “PRO.”

Earlier this week, Apple also confirmed that macOS 27.3 will introduce new checks for potentially harmful content shared through public iCloud links. Starting with the update, files and photos shared using the “Anyone with the link” option will be checked by Apple, which may remove flagged content or take action against accounts that violate its policies.

The release build for macOS 27.3 public beta 1 is 26C5035r, which is the same as the developer build.

If you’d like to join Apple’s free public beta program, visit beta.apple.com.

Google’s first Wyoming data centre will run on gas with carbon capture

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The $17B Cheyenne campus leans on a 702 MW fuel cell plant, with emissions stored underground.

The entrance to Google’s office at Pancras Square in London.

Google will build its first Wyoming data centre, a $17B investment near Cheyenne. Most of its power will come from natural gas, including a 702 MW fuel cell plant from AEP and Bloom Energy, with carbon capture meant to store most emissions underground.

Google will build its first data centre in Wyoming, a long-term $17B investment in Laramie County, the company said on Thursday. Much of its power will come from natural gas, with carbon capture meant to keep most of the emissions out of the air. It follows a $15B plan for Malaysia that Google announced on Wednesday.

The main source is a 702 MW fuel cell plant from American Electric Power, which turns natural gas into electricity without burning it. AEP said in October it would spend about $4B on the Bloom Energy plant near Cheyenne. Google will buy its power under a 20-year deal, POWER Magazine reported, and the plant should be fully running by the end of 2028.

Carbon from the fuel cells will be captured and stored underground at Tallgrass Energy’s nearby Eastern Wyoming Sequestration Hub. Google says this will remove “a large majority” of the plant’s emissions, but has not given a figure. It calls the plant the largest of its kind planned so far.

Google will also buy power from gas plants run by Tallgrass and Black Hills Energy, and is studying whether carbon capture can be fitted to Tallgrass’s turbines. With Wyoming, its contracted power from plants with carbon capture reaches nearly 1 GW. Its first such deal, for a gas plant in Illinois, came in October 2025.

Google says it will pay for all the power it uses and the grid upgrades its growth requires, something US lawmakers want Big Tech to do. The data centre will be air-cooled to save water.

Water is a sore point in Cheyenne. In July, the city halted some data centre water discharges after a Meta contractor released a rare bacterium into its wastewater reuse system.

Google expects the project to bring in hundreds of millions of dollars in property taxes for the county and its schools. It and its partners are also setting up a community council, after more than 200 residents raised worries about how data centre growth is changing Cheyenne. Google announced the project two days after US voters rejected data centres in 40 of 41 local ballot measures.

Muse has a voice mode now

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
